=== NOTES 1:0 | Sources of Ancient Indian History – Archaeological, Literary, Foreign Accounts; Dating Methods
## SUB 0 | Literary Sources – Works, Authors and Biographies
[0:0:0] # Most-asked: author → work
[0:0:1] TABLE head: Work | Author | Point to remember
  [0:0:1.0] Rajatarangini | Kalhana, 12th c., Sanskrit | Kings of Kashmir; 8 Tarangas, 7,826 verses; written 1148–49 under Jayasimha (Jai Singh, 1128–1149)
  [0:0:1.1] Arthashastra | Kautilya (Chanakya) | Mauryan polity and economy
  [0:0:1.2] Nitisara | Kamandaka (not Kautilya or Varahamihira) | Early book of politics (statecraft), Gupta age; followed across South and Southeast Asia
  [0:0:1.3] Mudrarakshasa | Vishakhadatta | Chandragupta Maurya and Chanakya vs the Nandas; court intrigues; names "Durabhisandhi"
  [0:0:1.4] Devichandraguptam | Vishakhadatta (not Bilhana) | Ramagupta – Chandragupta II; accession and marriage to Dhruvadevi
  [0:0:1.5] Hammira-Mahakavya | Nayachandra Suri (15th c. Jain) | Chauhan Hammira of Ranthambore
  [0:0:1.6] Nitivakyamrita | Somadeva Suri | Ethics and polity
  [0:0:1.7] Milinda-panha | Dialogue of Nagasena (not Nagarjuna) with Menander | Indo-Greek king Milinda
  [0:0:1.8] Kuvalayamala | Uddyotana Suri, 779 CE | Jain Prakrit campu
  [0:0:1.9] Harshacharita | Banabhatta | Biography of Harsha; calls Yajna Sri Satakarni "Trisamudradheesvara"
  [0:0:1.10] Vikramankadevacharita | Bilhana | Biography of Chalukya Vikramaditya VI
  [0:0:1.11] Brihatkatha | Gunadhya, Paisachi | Satavahana age
  [0:0:1.12] Gathasaptasati | King Hala (Satavahana), Maharashtri Prakrit | Satavahana society
  [0:0:1.13] Brihat Samhita | Varahamihira | –
  [0:0:1.14] Brihatkatha Kosha | Harisena | –
  [0:0:1.15] Samarangana Sutradhara | King Bhoja (not Pravarasena II) | –
  [0:0:1.16] Tilaka Manjari | Dhanapala, Jain poet at the Paramara court (Munja, Bhoja), c. 1000 CE | Sanskrit prose romance
  [0:0:1.17] Kashikhandam | Srinatha | Sea trade of the Reddy kingdom
  [0:0:1.18] Kavyamimamsa | Rajasekhara | –
  [0:0:1.19] Kavirajamarga | Rashtrakuta Amoghavarsha I | –
  [0:0:1.20] Muntakhab-ut-Tawarikh | Badauni | –
[0:0:2] - **Rajatarangini continued by:** **Jonaraja** (to Sultan Zainul Abedin) and his pupil **Srivara** – not Bilhana, Merutunga or Mammata.
[0:0:3] - **UPSC 2023 pairs (Devichandragupta–Bilhana, Hammira–Nayachandra, Milinda-panha–Nagarjuna, Nitivakyamrita–Somadeva):** **only two** correct (2 and 4).
[0:0:4] # Grammar and lexicon
[0:0:5] TABLE head: Work | Author
  [0:0:5.0] Ashtadhyayi – earliest known Sanskrit grammar | Panini, c. 6th–5th c. BC (PMF: 6th–4th c., most likely 5th c. BCE)
  [0:0:5.1] Varttika (on the Ashtadhyayi) | Katyayana
  [0:0:5.2] Mahabhashya | Patanjali – contemporary of Pushyamitra Shunga
  [0:0:5.3] Nirukta (etymology) | Yaska
  [0:0:5.4] Amarakosha (lexicon) | Amarasimha – a navaratna of Chandragupta II
[0:0:6] - **Navaratnas of Chandragupta II:** **Kalidasa**, Amarasimha, Varahamihira and others; Kalidasa = great Sanskrit poet of the Gupta age; Aryabhata wrote the Aryabhatiya.
[0:0:7] - **UPSC 2020 statement set:** "Panini – Pushyamitra" ✗ (Patanjali), "Amarasimha – Harsha" ✗ (Chandragupta II), "Kalidasa – Chandragupta II" ✓ → **3 only**.
[0:0:8] # Biographies vs chronicles
[0:0:9] - **Royal biographies (charitas):** Harshacharita (Bana), Vikramankadevacharita (Bilhana), Prithviraja Vijaya.
[0:0:10] - **Not a biography:** the **Dipavamsa** – a Sri Lankan **Pali chronicle** (4th–5th c. CE), like the Mahavamsa (NDA 2018 → "only I").
[0:0:11] # Other sources and periods
[0:0:12] - **Manusmriti (Manava Dharmashastra):** the **law book** of Manu – "Laws of Manu", a **Dharmashastra** on dharma, civil and criminal law and varna duties → answer "**law**".
[0:0:13]   - **⚠ Settled:** some keys print "social system" for UPPCS 2007; social order is its subject, but the text is a law code (PMF, New World Encyclopedia).
[0:0:14] - **Puranas (Vayu, Vishnu, Matsya):** king-lists of Mauryas and Satavahanas; Satavahana story in the **Vishnu Purana**; last Satavahana = **Pulomavi III** (not Gautamiputra).
[0:0:15] - **Sangam texts:** 3rd c. BCE – 3rd c. CE; Chera, Chola, Pandya.
[0:0:16] - **Rigveda:** composed in **Vedic Sanskrit** (Pali, Prakrit are later; Brahmi is a script).
[0:0:17] - **Magadhas (and Sutas):** court **bards** who memorised and recited chronicles, dynastic histories and epic tales (UPSC 2016) – Shramanas and Parivrajakas were ascetics.
[0:0:18] - **Dastan-i-Amir Hamza (Hamzanama)**, painted under Akbar: earliest Mughal illustrated manuscript.
[0:0:19] - **⚠ Trap:** Vishakhadatta wrote BOTH Mudrarakshasa (Mauryan) and Devichandraguptam (Gupta); the Mahabhashya is grammar, NOT about Chandragupta Maurya's rise.
[0:0:20] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 3 p.4, p.11, p.20; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.85; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.38, Part 2 p.32, p.37, p.61, Part 3 p.88, Part 4 p.7; PMF Art & Culture Part 4 p.53, Part 5 p.17; app notes 1:0 (sub 1)
## SUB 1 | Inscriptions – Mauryan, Kharavela, Rudradaman and Later
[0:1:0] # Earliest records
[0:1:1] TABLE head: Inscription | Ruler / issuer | Key facts
  [0:1:1.0] Boghaz-Koi (Asia Minor, Turkey) | Hittite–Mitanni treaty | 14th c. BC (c. 1380 BCE); names Indra, Varuna, Mitra, Nasatya; taken as evidence of Aryan movement via Iran
  [0:1:1.1] Sohgaura copper (bronze) plate (Gorakhpur, UP) | Mauryan | Earliest known copper plate; earliest royal order to preserve food grains for famine
  [0:1:1.2] Mahasthan (Bogura, Bangladesh) | Mauryan | Famine relief
[0:1:2] - **Boghaz-Koi** is important because **inscriptions mention Vedic gods** – not a trade centre, not where the Vedas were composed.
[0:1:3] # Kharavela – Hathigumpha
[0:1:4] TABLE head: Particular | Fact
  [0:1:4.0] Place | Udayagiri caves, near Bhubaneswar (Odisha)
  [0:1:4.1] King | Kharavela, Chedi / Mahameghavahana dynasty of Kalinga
  [0:1:4.2] Language | Prakrit, Brahmi; undated, damaged
  [0:1:4.3] Religion | Jain – salutes the Arhats/Jinas; gifts to Jain monks → Kharavela = patron of Jainism
  [0:1:4.4] Nandas | Kharavela extended a canal dug by a Nanda king ("Nandaraja", identified with Mahapadmananda) 300 years earlier → evidence of the Nandas
  [0:1:4.5] Other | 13 years of reign; wars incl. against Satakarni; names Tamil kingdoms; Krishna river = "Kannabenna"
[0:1:5] # Rudradaman – Junagadh (Girnar)
[0:1:6] - **Junagadh rock inscription of Rudradaman I** (c. 150 CE): **first long inscription in chaste Sanskrit**, **earliest specimen of Kavya style**; Brahmi; records his **achievements**, his conflict with the Satavahanas and his **repair of the Sudarshana lake without forced labour**.
[0:1:7] - **Sudarshana lake:** built under **Chandragupta Maurya** by governor **Pushyagupta**, canals under **Ashoka** (Tushaspha) → the inscription **names both Chandragupta and Ashoka** and **proves Chandragupta's sway over western India**.
[0:1:8] - **Same rock** also carries **Ashoka's 14 major rock edicts** (boulder near Uparkot fort) and a **Skandagupta** record (Parnadatta repaired the lake).
[0:1:9] - **⚠ Trap:** Rudradaman = **Junagadh**, not Nanaghat (Naganika).
[0:1:10] # Ashoka's edicts – what they mention
[0:1:11] - **Maski** (Karnataka): first edict naming "**Asoka**" (minor rock edict).
[0:1:12] - **Kalinga war:** told in **Major Rock Edict XIII** (not at Maski); Hathigumpha records Kharavela's own wars, not Ashoka's victory.
[0:1:13] - **Southern kingdoms named (RE II, XIII):** **Chola, Pandya, Satiyaputra, Keralaputra** – the **Satavahanas are NOT mentioned** (they rose after Ashoka).
[0:1:14] - **13th Major Rock Edict:** lists the **Andhras** among peoples inside the Mauryan empire.
[0:1:15] - **Yerragudi** (Kurnool): proves Mauryan rule in Andhra.
[0:1:16] # Other dynastic records
[0:1:17] TABLE head: Inscription | Issuer / composer | Content
  [0:1:17.0] Ayodhya (of Dhanadeva) | Pushyamitra Shunga | Two Ashvamedha sacrifices
  [0:1:17.1] Sarnath image | Kanishka | 81 AD, his 3rd year
  [0:1:17.2] Rabatak (Afghanistan, found 1993) | Kanishka | Bactrian language, Greek script; names Saket, Kaushambi, Pataliputra, Sri Champa – not Shravasti
  [0:1:17.3] Allahabad Pillar (Prayaga Prashasti) | Harisena for Samudragupta | Conquests; Sanskrit
  [0:1:17.4] Bhitari | Skandagupta | Repulse of the Hunas
  [0:1:17.5] Aihole | Ravikirti for Pulakeshin II | Harsha stopped at the Narmada; Sanskrit, Southern Brahmi
  [0:1:17.6] Lohnara | Pulakeshin II, 630 AD | Does NOT mention Harsha
  [0:1:17.7] Nanaghat | Queen Naganika | Early Satavahana history; "Rajan" Simuka
  [0:1:17.8] Nasik Prashasti | Gautami Balashri | Gautamiputra = "Ekabrahmana"; destroyer of Shakas, Yavanas, Pahlavas
  [0:1:17.9] Myakadoni (Bellary) | Pulumavi III | Basis of the Karnataka-homeland theory
  [0:1:17.10] Bhattiprolu | Raja Kubhiraka, 3rd c. BC | Nigama-sabha
  [0:1:17.11] Alluru | Ikshvaku period | Many donations to communities and institutions
  [0:1:17.12] Maidavolu plates | Pallava Sivaskandavarma | "Andhrapatha" first in an inscription
[0:1:18] - **Composer vs king:** Harisena → Samudragupta; Ravikirti → Pulakeshin II; Gautami Balashri → Gautamiputra Satakarni.
[0:1:19] - **Telugu firsts:** **Kalamalla** = first Telugu inscription (**Renati Chodas**); **Magallu** = first to mention the Kakatiyas; **Kazipet** = Beta Raja I the first Kakatiya king; first Telugu word "**Nagabu**" (Satavahana period).
[0:1:20] - Dashpur inscription: oldest advertisement tradition in India.
[0:1:21] ~~ Sources: Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.6-7; NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 2 p.47, Part 3 p.5, p.21; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.32, p.121; PMF Art & Culture Part 4 p.25; NextGen AP HISTORY Part 1 p.11; https://www.tutorialspoint.com/ashoka-s-inscription-describing-the-kalinga-war; app notes 1:0 (sub 3)
## SUB 2 | Scripts, Language of the Earliest Inscriptions and Decipherment
[0:2:0] # Language of the earliest inscriptions
[0:2:1] - **Earliest stone inscriptions = Ashoka's edicts**, in the **Prakrit language**, mostly in the **Brahmi script** – "Prakrit in Brahmi" (Brahmi is a script, not a language; not Sanskrit or Pali).
[0:2:2] - **Prakrit** = language of the earliest stone inscriptions and of the earliest AP inscriptions.
[0:2:3] # Scripts
[0:2:4] TABLE head: Script | Use / region | Decipherment
  [0:2:4.0] Brahmi | Most Ashokan edicts (Prakrit); left to right; parent of the Telugu script | James Prinsep, 1837, from Ashokan inscriptions
  [0:2:4.1] Kharosthi | Right to left; north-west (Gandhara); Ashokan Shahbazgarhi and Mansehra (Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, near Peshawar) – Prakrit in Kharosthi | Prinsep and others (Lassen, Norris), using bilingual Indo-Greek coins
  [0:2:4.2] Greek and Aramaic | Ashoka at Kandahar | –
  [0:2:4.3] Tamil-Brahmi (Tamizhi) | Earliest Tamil script, c. 3rd c. BCE | Mangulam, Sittanavasal, Arittapatti (Pandya region)
[0:2:5] - **Statement (UKPSC 2025):** "most Ashokan edicts are Prakrit in Brahmi" ✓ and "Mansehra, Shahbazgarhi are Prakrit in Kharosthi" ✓ → **both**.
[0:2:6] - **⚠ Trap:** Ashokan edicts are **not** in Sanskrit or Devanagari.
[0:2:7] # Decipherment
[0:2:8] - **James Prinsep** (Asiatic Society secretary) first deciphered **Brahmi (1837)** and read Ashoka's edicts – not Buhler, Max Muller, William Jones, Cunningham or A.H. Dani.
[0:2:9] - **Read from:** short inscriptions cut in **stone** (Sanchi donative records, rock and pillar edicts) – exam key "**stone tablets**"; coins helped only for Kharosthi.
[0:2:10] - **Ashoka identified:** George Turnour matched "Devanampiya Piyadasi" with Ashoka from the Dipavamsa and Mahavamsa.
[0:2:11] - **Pre-Ashokan Brahmi** (c. 4th c. BCE): **Anuradhapura** (Sri Lanka), Kodumanal and Keezhadi (Tamil Nadu); also Piprahwa, Sohgaura, Mahasthan.
[0:2:12] ~~ Sources: Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.3; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.26, p.42, Part 2 p.122; PMF Art & Culture Part 4 p.25; https://doubtnut.com/qna/648094099; app notes 1:0 (sub 4)
## SUB 3 | Foreign Accounts – Greek, Roman, Chinese and Arab
[0:3:0] # Greek and Roman writers
[0:3:1] TABLE head: Writer / work | Period | Key content
  [0:3:1.0] Herodotus – Historica (The Histories) | 5th c. BC | "Father of History"; Indo-Persian ties; first mention of "India"
  [0:3:1.1] Megasthenes – Indica | 4th c. BCE; envoy of Seleucus Nikator to Chandragupta Maurya, at Pataliputra | Mauryan administration, economy, urban life; Brahmins and Shramanas; first foreign work on Andhras: 30 fortified towns, 1,00,000 infantry
  [0:3:1.2] Periplus of the Erythraean Sea | c. 60 CE; anonymous Greek sailor (not Ctesias, Pliny or Ptolemy) | Andhra–Rome trade; Muziris and Arikamedu; Koddura only east-coast port; Saka–Satavahana conflict
  [0:3:1.3] Pliny – Natural History | Rome, Latin; 1st c. CE | Roman gold drain to India, about 50 million sesterces a year
  [0:3:1.4] Ptolemy – Geographia | 2nd c. CE | Masulia/Maisolia = Machilipatnam, textile market (not an army HQ); Ben Ra (Vengi)
[0:3:2] - **Temple of Augustus at Muziris:** shown on the **Peutinger Table (Tabula Peutingeriana)** – UPSC 2002 option "Peutingerian (garbled 'Genealogical') tables"; the Periplus describes Muziris as a port full of Arab and Greek ships but not the temple.
[0:3:3] - **⚠ Trap:** Indica is by **Megasthenes**, not Aryabhata; Megasthenes' Andhra forts = **30**.
[0:3:4] # Chinese visitors
[0:3:5] TABLE head: Traveller / work | Ruler / period | Key content
  [0:3:5.0] Fa-Hien (Faxian) – Fo-Kwo-Ki | Chandragupta II "Vikramaditya" | Buddhist pilgrimage; Gupta prosperity
  [0:3:5.1] Hiuen Tsang – Si-Yu-Ki | Harsha + Pulakeshin II; 630–645 CE | Studied at Nalanda; Mahayana; "King of Travellers"; Sriparvata vihara lacked patrons
  [0:3:5.2] I-Tsing | 671–695 CE, after Harsha | Landed at Tamralipti; Nalanda; monk life and relics
  [0:3:5.3] Matwalin (Ma-Twan-Lin) | – | China–India relations under the Chalukyas
[0:3:6] - **Chinese names for India:** "**Yin-tu**" (Yindu) or Shendu / Tien-chu – forms of Sindhu/Hindu (Fo-Kwo-Ki and Si-Yu-Ki are travel books).
[0:3:7] - **Order of visits:** **Megasthenes → Fa-Hien → Hiuen Tsang → I-Tsing → Al-Biruni** (UPSC 1999: 3, 1, 4, 2).
[0:3:8] - **⚠ Wrong pair (G1 2019):** **I-Tsing – Ashoka** – I-Tsing came in the late 7th c.; Fa-Hien – Chandragupta II, Al-Biruni – Mahmud, Ibn Battuta – Muhammad bin Tughluq are right.
[0:3:9] # Arab and later visitors
[0:3:10] TABLE head: Traveller / work | Period | Key content
  [0:3:10.0] Al-Masudi | 10th c. | Multan Sun Temple
  [0:3:10.1] Al-Biruni – Kitab-ul-Hind (Tahqiq-i-Hind) | Khwarizm; Arabic (not Persian); came with Mahmud of Ghazni, 11th c. | Hindu religion, Puranas, Sanskrit, Yoga; Kalachuri Gangeyadeva and capital Tripuri
  [0:3:10.2] Ibn Battuta – Rihla | Delhi 1333; qazi under Muhammad bin Tughlaq | Sultanate
[0:3:11] - **Al-Biruni statements:** learnt **Sanskrit** ✓, works shaped by the India of his day ✓, master of **trigonometry** ✓; "**secular writer**" ✗ – he was a devout Muslim scholar (UPPSC 2014).
[0:3:12] ~~ Sources: NextGen AP HISTORY Part 1 p.8, p.20; NextGen Satavahanas p.3; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.128, Part 2 p.122; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.21-22, Part 3 p.33, Part 4 p.25; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabula_Peutingeriana; https://doubtnut.com/qna/648094685; app notes 1:0 (sub 6)
[0:3:13] - **"Hindu" for the people of Hind:** of the usual options first used by the **Arabs** (al-Hind, Hindu); the word itself is the Persian form of Sindhu ("Hidu" in Achaemenid inscriptions); Greeks said "Indoi", Chinese "Shendu/Yindu".
## SUB 4 | Archaeologists, Historians and Discoveries
[0:4:0] TABLE head: Person | Contribution
  [0:4:0.0] Alexander Cunningham (1814–1893) | Bengal Engineers officer; first DG of ASI (1861); "Father of Indian Archaeology"; dug at Harappa (1853–75) but did not recognise its significance
  [0:4:0.1] James Prinsep | Deciphered Brahmi (1837)
  [0:4:0.2] John Marshall | DG of ASI; announced Indus Civilisation (1924); Mohenjodaro
  [0:4:0.3] Mortimer Wheeler | Arikamedu (1945); Harappa; invasion theory
  [0:4:0.4] James Burgess | Amaravati and Jaggayyapeta stupas
  [0:4:0.5] Alexander Rea | Bhattiprolu stupa
  [0:4:0.6] A.H. Longhurst | Nagarjunakonda excavations, after discovery by A. Ranganatha Saraswati (1926)
  [0:4:0.7] Walter Elliot | Madras civil servant; excavated Amaravati stupa (1845) – "Elliot Marbles"; wrote Coins of Southern India (1885)
  [0:4:0.8] Robert Sewell | A Forgotten Empire (1900) – the Vijayanagara empire
  [0:4:0.9] V. S. Sukthankar | Andhras and Satavahanas not the same people; Karnataka (Bellary) theory from Myakadoni
[0:4:1] - **UPSC 2023:** Rea, Longhurst, Sewell, Burgess and Elliot were all associated with **archaeological excavations** (not press, churches or railways).
[0:4:2] # Historians and books
[0:4:3] TABLE head: Historian | Known for
  [0:4:3.0] A.L. Basham | The Wonder That Was India
  [0:4:3.1] R.S. Sharma | Ancient Indian history (Satish Chandra – medieval; Bipan Chandra – modern)
  [0:4:3.2] Herodotus | "Father of History" (Thucydides – scientific history)
[0:4:4] - Parvataneni Veeraiah Chaudhary = "**Andhra Shivaji**". Annamacharya = "**Padakavita Pitamaha**". Karikala Chola: **Kallanai** (Grand Anicut) on the Kaveri. Kundakundacharya: **Syadvada**.
[0:4:5] # Places and recent finds
[0:4:6] - **Ancient name → state (UPSC 2020):** **Bhilsa (Vidisha) – Madhya Pradesh** ✓, **Girinagar (Junagadh) – Gujarat** ✓; Dwarasamudra (Halebidu) is in Karnataka and Sthaneshwar (Thanesar) in Haryana → "I and III only".
[0:4:7] - **Nov 2016:** a city over **7,000 years** old was unearthed near Abydos in **Egypt** (WBCS 2017).
[0:4:8] - **14 Jun 2026:** field site (Satavahana to Vijayanagara) in the **Nallamala Tiger Reserve**, Yerragondapalem region.
[0:4:9] - **Apr 2026:** Tamil-Brahmi traders' names (Cikai Korran, Kopan, Catan), 1st–3rd c. CE, identified in Egypt's **Valley of the Kings**.
[0:4:10] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History APPSC Part 6 p.71; NextGen AP HISTORY Part 6 p.11, Part 13 p.8; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.42, Part 2 p.95; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harappa; https://www.clearias.com/ (UPSC 2023 key); app notes 1:0 (sub 7)
## SUB 5 | Coins As A Source
[0:5:0] TABLE head: Series | Point
  [0:5:0.0] Punch-marked (silver) | Earliest extant Indian coins (c. 6th c. BCE)
  [0:5:0.1] Indo-Greek | First with rulers' names and portraits
  [0:5:0.2] Kushana | First to issue gold coins on a large scale, regularly (1st c. CE)
  [0:5:0.3] Gupta | Largest number of gold coins (dinaras)
  [0:5:0.4] Satavahana | Lead, potin, copper, bronze, some silver – not gold; Karpanamulu = copper coins; no nickel; ship-motif coins of Yajna Sri and Vasishthiputra Pulumavi; Gautamiputra re-struck Nahapana's coins
  [0:5:0.5] Kalachuri | Gold, silver and copper
  [0:5:0.6] Ikshvaku | Santamula I gave away crores of gold coins
  [0:5:0.7] Kakatiya | Gold Mada (not Satavahana); Nishkam, Tankam, Varaha
[0:5:1] - **Wrong pair:** "**Satavahanas – gold**".
[0:5:2] - **Chronology (UPSC 1998):** urban culture (Harappan) → iron plough → punch-marked silver coins → gold coins = **IV, III, II, I**.
[0:5:3] - First clear textual coin terms: **kahapana, nikkha, kamsa, kakanika** in **Pali** texts.
[0:5:4] - Apr 2026: ASI began excavating **Balirajgarh** (Madhubani, **Bihar**) – punch-marked coins, Mauryan-to-Pala habitation.
[0:5:5] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.33, Part 3 p.2; NextGen AP HISTORY Part 1 p.13; Rocket Indian History Part 2 p.41; app notes 1:0 (sub 5)
## SUB 6 | Religious Literature As A Source
[0:6:0] TABLE head: Text | What it reveals
  [0:6:0.0] Aitareya Brahmana | Earliest literary reference to Andhras (Sunahshepa story – Vishwamitra's rejected sons)
  [0:6:0.1] Vinayavatthu | First Buddhist mention of "Andhrapatha"
  [0:6:0.2] Bhimasena, Serivanija Jatakas | Andhranagari (Andhakapura) = Dhanyakataka; P. C. Ray saw its river as the Krishna
  [0:6:0.3] Chulakalinga Jataka | Kalinga–Assaka conflict; Assaka capital Potali
  [0:6:0.4] Milindapanho | Dialogue of Menander I (Milinda) with monk Nagasena
  [0:6:0.5] Dipavamsa, Mahavamsa | Sri Lankan Pali chronicles; Mauryan history; Andhra Buddhism
  [0:6:0.6] Nayadhammakahao (Jain) | Ikshvaku ruler Yashodhara and son Dhanada
[0:6:1] - **Earliest Jain literature:** in **Ardha-Magadhi** (twelve Angas), finally compiled in the 6th c. AD at **Vallabhi**; earliest Jain holy book: **Fourteen Purvas**. Jain: Kalpasutra.
[0:6:2] - Mahabharata: Andhras fought for the **Kauravas**. Buddhist name for the region: "**Andhakaratta**".
[0:6:3] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 2 p.61; NextGen AP History Previous Papers p.8; https://www.britannica.com/topic/Jainism; app notes 1:0 (sub 2)
## SUB 7 | Types of Sources and Dating Methods
[0:7:0] - Three groups: **archaeological** (inscriptions, coins, monuments, sites), **literary** (religious and secular texts) and **foreign accounts**.
[0:7:1] - **Inscriptions** are the **most reliable** source; foreign accounts only **supplement** Indian sources.
[0:7:2] - **Earliest land-grant records:** **Satavahana**.
[0:7:3] TABLE head: Method | Principle
  [0:7:3.0] Stratigraphy | Superposition: lower layer is older
  [0:7:3.1] Radiocarbon (C-14) | Decay of C-14 in organic remains; half-life about 5,730 years
  [0:7:3.2] Dendrochronology | Counting annual tree rings
  [0:7:3.3] Thermoluminescence | Light given on heating pottery/burnt material
[0:7:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.42; app notes 1:0 (sub 0, 8)

Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  *1 only | 2 only | Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: Kharosthi runs right to left. Prinsep read Brahmi from Ashokan inscriptions; bilingual Indo-Greek coins helped decipher Kharosthi.

  x: Ashoka's edict at Kandahar is in Greek and Aramaic.

  x: The standard figure associated with Megasthenes is 30 fortified towns and an infantry force of one lakh. The alternative figure of 80 forts is treated as a textual variation, not the standard figure.
[nd1e0e49863c] sub3

  x: Masulia or Maisolia, identified with Machilipatnam, was described by Ptolemy as a great trading centre. It was not a Roman military headquarters.
[n410557144a3] sub3

  x: Pliny complained that Roman gold was flowing to India in payment for Indian spices, pearls, muslin and other goods. His own figure is commonly given as about 50 million sesterces annually.

=== NOTES 1:1 | Sources for the History of Andhra – Inscriptions, Coins, Literature and Foreign Accounts
## SUB 0 | Satavahana Inscriptions – Language, Script and Issuers
[1:0:0] # Language and script – the most-asked fact
[1:0:1] - **All Satavahana inscriptions:** **Prakrit language, Brahmi script** (G2 2002; APPSC PYQ ×2) – not "Sanskrit language, Prakrit script" or "Brahmi language".
[1:0:2] - **Royal language:** **Prakrit** – the administrative, business, written and royal language (G2 2011); Sanskrit and Telugu are distractors.
[1:0:3] - **Script:** **Brahmi** (not Kharosthi, Greek or Aramaic).
[1:0:4] # Issuer → record
[1:0:5] TABLE head: Inscription | Dynasty / issuer | What it records
  [1:0:5.0] Naneghat | Queen Naganika, wife of Satakarni I | Prakrit; Vedic sacrifices; gifts of cows, land, gold to Brahmins; label "Raya Simuka Satavahana" – first Satavahana with the title Rajan
  [1:0:5.1] Nasik | Gautami Balasri, in the reign of Vasishthiputra Pulumavi | Prakrit prose; victories and titles of Gautamiputra Satakarni: "Ekabrahmana", "Kshatriya-darpa-mana-mardana", destroyer of Shakas, Yavanas, Pahlavas
  [1:0:5.2] Amaravati | Pulumavi II | First Satavahana inscription in Andhra; word "Nagabu"
  [1:0:5.3] Myakadoni (Bellary) | Pulumavi III | Fall of the kingdom; officer "Gulmika"; Telugu word "Vepuru"
  [1:0:5.4] Malkapuram (1261 CE) | Kakatiya, Rudramadevi's reign | Musical instrument "Jalakaranda"
[1:0:6] - **⚠ Wrong pair (APPSC, G1):** **Rudradaman – Nanaghat**; Rudradaman's record is **Junagadh**. Gautami Balasri – Nasik, Kharavela – Hathigumpha, Ashoka – Maski are right.
[1:0:7] - Naneghat and Nasik lie in **Maharashtra**.
[1:0:8] - **Gulmika:** officer over a group of villages – the **village headman** of the Satavahana age (gramani in Mauryan times); also a border/feudatory chief (Myakadoni). The question's "Maski (Macedonian)" means **Myakadoni**.
[1:0:9] ~~ Sources: Rocket AP History p.16, p.52; NextGen AP HISTORY Part 1 p.12, p.15, p.23, Part 2 p.2, p.12; NextGen Satavahanas p.10; app notes 1:1 (sub 2)
## SUB 1 | Mauryan and Pre-Satavahana Records – Erragudi, Bhattiprolu, Hathigumpha
[1:1:0] # Earliest records of Andhra
[1:1:1] - **Earliest inscriptions on Andhra history:** Ashoka's edicts at **Yerragudi (Erragudi)** and **Rajula Mandagiri** (Kurnool) – **Prakrit** language, Brahmi script (G1 1984, 1987).
[1:1:2] - **Proof of Mauryan rule in Andhra:** **Yerragudi** inscription (not Maski, which is in Karnataka).
[1:1:3] - **Erragudi:** Ashoka tells **teachers to inculcate Dhamma in pupils** (Welfare Officers flashcard).
[1:1:4] # Bhattiprolu (Guntur)
[1:1:5] TABLE head: Particular | Fact
  [1:1:5.0] Date | 3rd c. BC, relic caskets of the oldest stupa in AP
  [1:1:5.1] King | Raja Kubhiraka (Kubheraka / Kuberaka), a local pre-Satavahana ruler
  [1:1:5.2] Body named | Nigama-sabha (town council / guild assembly) (G2 2011 ×2)
  [1:1:5.3] Other | Among the oldest Prakrit inscriptions of Andhra; Roman coins found
[1:1:6] - **⚠ Trap:** Nanaghat, Erragudi and Amaravati do not mention the Nigama-sabha; Ashoka, Mahadeva and Sudarshana did not rule Bhattiprolu.
[1:1:7] # Kharavela and the Krishna
[1:1:8] - **Hathigumpha inscription:** in his 2nd year Kharavela sent an army west, ignoring **Satakarni**, to the **Kannabenna** river (= **Krishna**) and attacked the Mushika city.
[1:1:9] ~~ Sources: NextGen AP HISTORY Part 1 p.6, p.11, Part 2 p.2-3, Part 6 p.14, Part 7 p.7; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.42; app notes 1:1 (sub 2)
## SUB 2 | Ikshvaku, Pallava, Brihatphalayana and Salankayana Inscriptions
[1:2:0] # Issuer and record
[1:2:1] TABLE head: Inscription | Issuer | Records
  [1:2:1.0] Nagarjunakonda | Ikshvaku (Veerapurushadatta) | Upasika Bodhisri; Buddhist patronage; Santamula founded the kingdom at Vijayapuri
  [1:2:1.1] Jaggayyapeta | Ikshvaku, Virapurushadatta's reign | Buddhist donations
  [1:2:1.2] Alluru | Ikshvaku period | Multiple gifts to different sections and institutions by the ruler (G2 2017) – the Buddhist community got bullock carts
  [1:2:1.3] Chejerla | Kandararaju | Ikshvaku – Anandagotrika wars
  [1:2:1.4] Manchikallu | Pallava Simhavarman, near Vijayapuri | Conquest of Vijayapuri after defeating the Ikshvakus (G2 2019)
  [1:2:1.5] Mayidavolu (c. AD 300) | Pallava Sivaskandavarman | Prakrit copper-plate grant; first inscriptional use of "Andhrapatha"
  [1:2:1.6] Kondamudi (copper) | Jayavarma, Brihatphalayana, 10th regnal year | Only record of the Brihatphalayanas (Ikshvaku feudatories); capital Koduru; village to Brahmins; Prakrit language, seal and subject in Sanskrit
  [1:2:1.7] Pedavegi | Nandi Varma II (Salankayana) | Land grant to Vishnu; first to mention Vishnu temples in Andhra
  [1:2:1.8] Kolleru | Nandi Varma II | –
  [1:2:1.9] Eluru | Devavarma | "Munyada" = political employee
  [1:2:1.10] Pithapuram | – | Mentions Kolleru lake
  [1:2:1.11] Kalamalla | Erikal Mutthuraju Dhananjaya Varma | First inscription in Telugu (not Sanskrit)
  [1:2:1.12] Allahabad Pillar | Samudragupta | Names Hastivarman of Vengi (Salankayana) among southern kings defeated
[1:2:2] - **Ikshvaku inscriptions:** **Prakrit** was their official and inscriptional language (Asst. BC Welfare 2017); Sanskrit got patronage only from **Ehuvala Santamula**'s time.
[1:2:3] - **⚠ Trap:** **Manchikallu and Mayidavolu = Pallavas**, not Ikshvakus (G2 Mains 2025); Karle, Veerapuram and Ramagiri are distractors for Alluru.
[1:2:4] # Fall of the Ikshvakus
[1:2:5] - Last Ikshvaku ruler **Rudrapurushadatta** (son of Ehuvala Chamtamula); his Nagarjunakonda pillar (11th year) honours his mother Vammabhatta; the **Phanigiri** pillar is of his 18th year.
[1:2:6] - Defeated by **Pallava Simhavarman** (Manchikallu), not by the Vishnukundins.
[1:2:7] - **South of the Krishna → Pallavas**; north → Brihatphalayanas.
[1:2:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen AP HISTORY Part 3 p.1, Part 8 p.12-13; NextGen Ikshvakus p.5; NextGen AP History Previous Papers p.8, p.52, p.70; Rocket AP History p.16, p.52; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allahabad_Pillar; app notes 1:1 (sub 3)
## SUB 3 | Literary Sources for Andhra
[1:3:0] TABLE head: Text | Content revealed
  [1:3:0.0] Matsya Purana | 30 (29 in some counts) Satavahana kings ruled for 456 years
  [1:3:0.1] Vishnu Purana | Story of the Satavahanas
  [1:3:0.2] Bhimasena Jataka | "Andhapatha" (Andhra route); Serivanija Jataka = Andhapura on the Telavaha
  [1:3:0.3] Chulla-Kalinga Jataka | Kharavela defeated by Asmaka king Satakarni
  [1:3:0.4] Vinayavatthu | "Andhapatha"
  [1:3:0.5] Gathasaptasati (King Hala) | Maharashtri Prakrit, 700 verses; society; early Telugu words "atta, addam, potta"
  [1:3:0.6] Pratishtana Pura Kalpa, Dwatrimsatputtalika, Kathasaritsagara | Early history of the Satavahana family
  [1:3:0.7] Kashikhandam and Haravilasam | Both by Srinatha; sea trade of the Reddi period
  [1:3:0.8] Nritta Ratnavali | Jayapa Senani (Ganapatideva's Gaja Sahini) – Sanskrit treatise on dance
[1:3:1] - **Not a source of early Satavahana history:** the **Gathasaptasati** (G2 Mains 2017) – it is a verse anthology.
[1:3:2] - **Andhakaratta:** the name of the Andhras in **Buddhist** literature (G2 Mains 2017) – not Jain or Vedic.
[1:3:3]   - **⚠ Settled:** the printed key "Jain literature" is wrong (NextGen Previous Papers p.8).
[1:3:4] - **Chilukuri Veerabhadra Rao:** wrote **Andhrula Charitramu (The History of the Andhras)**, which carried knowledge of the Andhras' past across Andhra; Kanyasulkam is Gurajada Apparao's.
[1:3:5] - Reddi sea-trade question has two keys: Kashikhandam (G2 2011) and Haravilasam (G2 2019) – both by Srinatha.
[1:3:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Satavahanas p.1, p.5; NextGen Kakatiyas p.31; NextGen AP History Previous Papers p.8, p.10; https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chilukuri_Veerabhadra_Rao; app notes 1:1 (sub 1)
## SUB 4 | Coins and Trade Evidence
[1:4:0] - **Ship coins:** the anchored-ship (ship-mast) coins of **Yajnasri Satakarni** prove brisk **maritime (naval) trade** (APPSC PYQ; Special Qualifying Test 2018); **Vasishthiputra Pulumavi** also issued ship-motif coins.
[1:4:1]   - **⚠ Trap:** the Periplus, Nasik and Karle records are not the evidence asked for.
[1:4:2] - Satavahana coins: **lead, potin, copper, bronze** and silver; gold was not their coinage.
[1:4:3] - **Roman coins** at Andhra sites belong to the **Satavahana** period (APSLPRB 2019) and show **Andhra–Rome trade**.
[1:4:4] TABLE head: Hoard | Find-spots
  [1:4:4.0] Roman gold | Attirala (Kadapa), Vinukonda (Guntur)
  [1:4:4.1] Others | Salihundam, Vijayawada, Nagarjunakonda, Nellore, Bhattiprolu
[1:4:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Satavahanas p.19, p.21; NextGen AP HISTORY Part 1 p.13, Part 2 p.8; app notes 1:1 (sub 5, 7)
## SUB 5 | Copper-plate Grants and Land Grants
[1:5:0] - **Donor** = king; **donee** = Brahmins; gift of a **village/land**, often with **tax exemptions**.
[1:5:1] - **Earliest surviving land grant with fiscal exemptions:** the **Satavahanas** (Naneghat; Gautamiputra's Nasik grants) – not the Guptas (Asst. Tribal Welfare Officer PYQ).
[1:5:2] - **Vishnukundin** grants measured land in **nivartanas**.
[1:5:3] - Examples: Mayidavolu (Pallava), Kondamudi (Brihatphalayana).
[1:5:4] TABLE head: Term | Meaning
  [1:5:4.0] nivartana | Land measure (Vishnukundin grants)
  [1:5:4.1] Nigama-sabha | Town council (Bhattiprolu)
  [1:5:4.2] Munyada | Political employee (Eluru)
  [1:5:4.3] Jalakaranda | Musical instrument (Malkapuram)
[1:5:5] ~~ Sources: Upinder Singh, A History of Ancient and Early Medieval India ch. 8; app notes 1:1 (sub 4)
## SUB 6 | Archaeological Sites and Foreign Accounts on Andhra
[1:6:0] - Satavahana history rests on the **Puranas**, **Prakrit inscriptions** and **coins**; foreign accounts must be cross-checked against these.
[1:6:1] TABLE head: Type | Sites
  [1:6:1.0] Stupa sites | Amaravati, Nagarjunakonda, Bhattiprolu (oldest), Jaggayyapeta, Guntupalli, Salihundam
  [1:6:1.1] Habitation mounds | Gudiwada Dibba (Mauryan to Satavahana), Kotilingala, Kondapur (mint), Dharanikota
[1:6:2] - Dharanikota is a habitation mound, not a listed stupa site.
[1:6:3] # Foreign accounts on Andhra
[1:6:4] TABLE head: Writer | Period | Andhra places / facts
  [1:6:4.0] Megasthenes | 4th c. BCE, Mauryan | Andhras: 30 fortified towns
  [1:6:4.1] Periplus | c. 60 CE | Koddura port; Saka–Satavahana conflict
  [1:6:4.2] Pliny | 1st c. CE | Roman gold drain to India
  [1:6:4.3] Ptolemy | 2nd c. CE | Masulia (Machilipatnam), Ben Ra (Vengi)
  [1:6:4.4] Hiuen Tsang | 7th c. CE (visited Dhanakataka, c. 639 CE) | Buddhist monasteries of Andhra
[1:6:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen AP HISTORY Part 1 p.8; app notes 1:1 (sub 0, 6, 8)

  Kaveri | *Telavaha | Mahanadi | Narmada
  x: The Serivanija Jataka places Andhapura on the Telavaha river.
[f23d1fc945c74] sub4

  *Vasishthiputra Pulumavi and Yajna Sri | Satakarni I and Hala | Gautamiputra Satakarni and Simuka | Pulumavi II and Satakarni I
  x: Ship-motif coins of Vasishthiputra Pulumavi and Yajna Sri prove Satavahana maritime trade.

  x: Satavahana coins were issued in lead, copper, bronze, potin and silver, so 'never in silver' is wrong.

  x: Roman coins in Andhra show Andhra–Rome trade.

  x: Vishnukundin grants measured land in ‘nivartanas’.

=== NOTES 1:2 | Stone Age Cultures – Palaeolithic, Mesolithic, Neolithic, Chalcolithic Sites of India
## SUB 0 | Neolithic – First Farming, Pit Dwellings, Dog Burials, Ash Mounds
[2:0:0] # First farming
[2:0:1] - **Cultivation of cereals began in the Neolithic.**
[2:0:2] - **Mehrgarh** (Baluchistan, c. **7000 BCE**): **earliest agriculture** (wheat, barley) and settled life in the subcontinent; of dated options "7000 BC" is the start of farming.
[2:0:3] - **Rice:** **Lahuradeva** (UP, c. **6500 BCE**, oldest paddy); **Koldihwa** (Belan valley, UP) – earlier thought oldest; sequence **Neolithic–Chalcolithic–Iron**.
[2:0:4] # Site table
[2:0:5] TABLE head: Site | Region | Distinctive find
  [2:0:5.0] Burzahom | Kashmir (16 km NW of Srinagar) | Pit dwellings; dog buried with master
  [2:0:5.1] Gufkral | Kashmir | Pit dwellings (with Burzahom)
  [2:0:5.2] Chirand | Bihar | Bone and antler tools
  [2:0:5.3] Koldihwa | Belan valley, UP | Early rice
  [2:0:5.4] Sanganakallu | Ballari, Karnataka | Ash mound (also Utnur, Piklihal, Kupgal, Budihal)
  [2:0:5.5] Tekkalakota | Ballari, Karnataka | Hill-dwellers; excavated by M. S. Nagaraja Rao
  [2:0:5.6] Brahmagiri, Maski | Karnataka | Neolithic–megalithic sites
[2:0:6] - **Ash mounds:** burnt dung of cattle pens = seasonal camps of Neolithic herdsmen.
[2:0:7] - **Eastern Neolithic** (Birbhanpur, Chirand, Kuchai, Sankarjang): **pointed-butt celts** and **shouldered axes**.
[2:0:8] - **Pit dwellings** are NOT at Mehrgarh (mud-brick houses), Pallavaram (Palaeolithic tools) or Balakot (Harappan).
[2:0:9] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.53-58; NextGen Indian History Part 1 p.4; Rocket Indian History p.104; app notes 1:2
[2:0:10] - **UPSC pairs:** **Chandraketugarh** (West Bengal) = **terracotta art**; **Ganeshwar** (Rajasthan, Khetri belt) = **copper artefacts**; Burzahom = pit dwellings, not rock-cut shrines → "2 and 3".
## SUB 1 | Mesolithic – Domestication, Burials and Ganga–Vindhya Sites
[2:1:0] # Most-asked facts
[2:1:1] - **Animal domestication began in the Mesolithic:** earliest evidence at **Adamgarh** (Narmadapuram, MP) and **Bagor** (Bhilwara, Rajasthan) – not Langhnaj, Birbhanpur or Chopani Mando.
[2:1:2] - **Mahadaha** (Pratapgarh, UP): **bone and antler ornaments** (earrings, necklaces), bone implements, double burials.
[2:1:3] - **Damdama** (Pratapgarh): 41 graves, five double burials and one **triple burial** (three skeletons in one grave).
[2:1:4] - **Sarai Nahar Rai** (Pratapgarh): a grave with **four** skeletons.
[2:1:5] - **Lekhahia** (Mirzapur, Vindhyas): **17 human skeletons** from rock shelter 1 – the most from any Vindhyan rock shelter.
[2:1:6] # Sites and regions
[2:1:7] TABLE head: Site | Region | Note
  [2:1:7.0] Bagor | Bhilwara, Rajasthan | Rock shelters, microliths, early domestication
  [2:1:7.1] Adamgarh | Narmadapuram, MP | Rock shelter, early domestication
  [2:1:7.2] Langhnaj | Gujarat | Microliths, burials
  [2:1:7.3] Sarai Nahar Rai, Mahadaha, Damdama | Ganga plain, Pratapgarh (UP) | Lake-side camps, burials
  [2:1:7.4] Lekhahia, Baghai Khor, Morahana Pahar, Chopani Mando | Vindhyas / Belan valley (Mirzapur, UP) | Rock shelters; Chopani Mando – huts, microliths
  [2:1:7.5] Paisra | Bihar | –
  [2:1:7.6] Birbhanpur | West Bengal | –
  [2:1:7.7] Teri sites | Thoothukudi (TN) and Visakhapatnam coast | Coastal red-sand dunes
[2:1:8] - **West to east:** **Mahadaha → Lekhahia → Paisra → Birbhanpur**.
[2:1:9] > Mesolithic = microlithic stage; tools made by **flaking and chipping**.
[2:1:10] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.51-53; iaspoint (Mahadaha, Mesolithic burials); Wikipedia "Lekhahia"; app notes 1:2
## SUB 2 | Bhimbetka and Prehistoric Rock Art
[2:2:0] - **Bhimbetka:** foothills of the Vindhyas, **Raisen** district near Bhopal (MP); discovered by **V. S. Wakankar in 1957-58**; **UNESCO World Heritage Site 2003**.
[2:2:1] - **Most rock paintings in India** (up to 20 layers in places); Upper Palaeolithic art in about **300** caves and shelters; continuous use from Lower Palaeolithic to medieval times.
[2:2:2] - Paintings are **Upper Palaeolithic and Mesolithic** – hunting scenes, animals, geometric patterns; Ajanta, Ellora, Bagh are historic-period cave art, not prehistoric.
[2:2:3] - Most commonly painted animal: **deer**. A **petroglyph** is **carved** into rock; a painting is applied on the surface.
[2:2:4] - 12 May 2026 study: after contact with the Chalcolithic farmers of **Malwa**, the Bhimbetka style became **static**.
[2:2:5] - H. D. Sankalia, S. S. Nigam, R. Pandey, M. S. Vats, V. N. Misra are distractors – the discoverer is **Wakankar**.
[2:2:6] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.59-61; app notes 1:2 (sub 6)
## SUB 3 | Chalcolithic Cultures – Ahar, Kayatha, Malwa, Jorwe
[2:3:0] - **Chalcolithic = Copper Age** (copper + stone tools); **first metal used = copper**.
[2:3:1] - The **Chalcolithic** people are credited with the first wide use of **painted pottery** (a few Neolithic sites had some).
[2:3:2] TABLE head: Culture | Sites | Region | Date | Pottery / feature
  [2:3:2.0] Ahar-Banas | Ahar, Gilund, Balathal | Mewar, Banas valley (Rajasthan) | c. 2800/2100–1500 BCE | White-painted black-and-red ware; copper smelting
  [2:3:2.1] Kayatha | Kayatha (Chambal) | MP | 2450–1700 BCE | Chocolate-slipped ware
  [2:3:2.2] Malwa | Navdatoli (Narmada), Eran | Madhya Pradesh | 1900–1400 BCE | Malwa ware; Navdatoli dug by H. D. Sankalia
  [2:3:2.3] Jorwe | Jorwe, Nevasa, Daimabad, Inamgaon | Maharashtra (nuclear zone Pravara–Godavari) | 1400–700 BCE | Painted red ware (black-on-red)
[2:3:3] - **Contemporaries of the Harappans:** **Ahar-Banas** and Kayatha; Malwa and Jorwe are later. Order of start: Ahar → Kayatha → Malwa → Jorwe.
[2:3:4] - **Burials in Maharashtra:** dead buried in urns **under the house floor, north to south**.
[2:3:5] - **Daimabad** (Pravara): largest Jorwe site; **bronze chariot**, buffalo, elephant, rhinoceros; **symbolic burial**; southernmost Late Harappan site.
[2:3:6] - **Jorwe end:** most sites abandoned gradually after c. 1000 BCE (drought); Inamgaon, Prakash continued (Late Jorwe) – "**suddenly deserted**" is the wrong statement (CDS 2012).
[2:3:7] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.63-74; examrobot CDS-I 2012 key; app notes 1:2 (sub 5)
## SUB 4 | Stone Age Framework – Phases, Proto-history, Early Man
[2:4:0] - **Order:** Lower → Middle → Upper Palaeolithic → Mesolithic → Neolithic → Chalcolithic → Iron Age (Megalithic).
[2:4:1] - **Phases are defined by tool type and technology**; tools varied by region and cultures did **not** evolve uniformly or unilinearly.
[2:4:2] - **Three-Age system** (Stone, Bronze, Iron): **C. J. Thomsen** (Copenhagen museum); "Palaeolithic/Neolithic" coined by Lubbock.
[2:4:3] - **Proto-history:** writing exists but is undeciphered – the **Indus civilisation** is **proto-historic**.
[2:4:4] - **Early man:** **Narmada Man** skull found by **Arun Sonakia, 1982** at **Hathnora** (Narmada valley, MP) – earliest human fossil of India; Bori (Maharashtra) artefacts c. 1.38 million years.
[2:4:5] - **Cro-Magnon** = anatomically modern humans of the Upper Palaeolithic – later than Neanderthals and Heidelberg man.
[2:4:6] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.26-27, p.45-47; Britannica (Three-Age system, Cro-Magnon)
[2:4:7] - **Wheel:** first invented c. **3500 BC** (Mesopotamia); in India solid-wheeled carts and toy carts appear with the Harappans.
[2:4:8] - **Why so many Stone Age sites in the South:** the Deccan and Andhra hills had **abundant stone (quartzite) for tools** and rocky terrain that preserves sites, while thick Gangetic alluvium buries them.
## SUB 5 | Megaliths – Nature, Iron Age and New Finds
[2:5:0] - **Megaliths:** graves with weapons, tools and pottery, marked by **big stones** (mega = large, lith = stone) – **burial sites** away from homes; not microliths, chalcoliths or monoliths.
[2:5:1] - Belong to the **Iron Age**, with **black-and-red ware**; c. 1000 BCE – 2nd century BCE (PMF).
[2:5:2] - Earliest AMS-dated iron: **Mayiladumparai** (Krishnagiri, TN) – **2172 BCE**.
[2:5:3] - **Bihar:** first documentation of a megalithic site (July 2019) in the **Kaimur hills** (Hurmeta menhirs).
[2:5:4] - **Kerala:** rock-cut burial chamber at **Panayal** (Kasaragod).
[2:5:5] - 21 Jul 2026: **Karivalamvanthanallur** (Tenkasi, TN) passed straight from **Microlithic to Iron/Early Historic**, with no Neolithic or Chalcolithic.
[2:5:6] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 3 p.27-28; rohtas.nic.in (megalithic tombs); app notes 1:2 (sub 8-9)
## SUB 6 | Palaeolithic – Tool Techniques and Sites
[2:6:0] TABLE head: Stage | Technique / tools | Sites
  [2:6:0.0] Lower | Acheulian = handaxe-cleaver; Soanian = chopper-chopping | Attirampakkam (TN), Hunsgi and Isampur (Karnataka), Didwana (Rajasthan), Sohan valley (Pakistan Punjab), Belan valley
  [2:6:0.1] Middle | Flake tools; prepared-core Levallois technique; scrapers, borers, points | Nevasa, Bhimbetka, Narmada valley; Sembur (TN)
  [2:6:0.2] Upper | Blades, burins, bone tools | Kurnool caves, Jwalapuram, Patne, Baghor
[2:6:1] - **Attirampakkam** is an **Old Stone Age** (Lower Palaeolithic, Acheulian) site – not "Middle Stone Age" (Mesolithic).
[2:6:2] - **Levallois** = Middle Palaeolithic prepared-core flake technique (Sembur); "Lacoid/Placoid/Hollois" are not techniques. No Neanderthal fossil has been found in India.
[2:6:3] - Middle Palaeolithic = **flake** stage, not the microlithic stage.
[2:6:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.47-50; app notes 1:2 (sub 1)

  x: Kayatha is in Madhya Pradesh; Jorwe, Savalda and Inamgaon are in Maharashtra.

=== NOTES 1:3 | Pre-historic Cultures of Andhra – Palaeolithic, Mesolithic, Neolithic and Megalithic Sites
## SUB 0 | Palaeolithic Andhra – Billa Surgam, Kurnool Caves and Sites
[3:0:0] # Billa Surgam and the Kurnool caves (most asked)
[3:0:1] - **Billa Surgam caves:** K. K. Kottala village, **Betamcherla** mandal, **Kurnool** (now Nandyal) district – **not Anantapuramu**; limestone caves; **Old Stone Age** site.
[3:0:2] - Explored by **Robert Bruce Foote** (1884) – the "Father of Indian prehistory", who also found the first Palaeolithic tool at **Pallavaram** (Chennai); **stone and rare bone tools** with Pleistocene animal fossils.
[3:0:3] - **Kurnool caves** and **Muchatla Chintamani Gavi**: the **only Indian sites with bone tools in an Upper Palaeolithic context**.
[3:0:4] - **Belum Caves** (Kolimigundla, Nandyal): **second-longest** cave system in India.
[3:0:5] - **Betamcherla** = famous for **prehistoric caves** (not temples or a dam).
[3:0:6] - Distractors in the G2 2017 question: **Keesarapalli** (Chalcolithic), **Utnur** (Neolithic ash mound), **Revarala** (megalithic menhir).
[3:0:7] # Sites by district
[3:0:8] TABLE head: District | Palaeolithic sites
  [3:0:8.0] Kurnool | Billa Surgam, Betamcherla, Veerapuram, Sangameswaram, Jwalapuram (Upper Palaeolithic)
  [3:0:8.1] Prakasam | Giddaluru, Dornala, Yerragondapalem (well-made quartzite blades), Nandikanuma
  [3:0:8.2] Guntur / Krishna valley | Nagarjunakonda, Karampudi, Chejarla
  [3:0:8.3] Chittoor | Renigunta (Upper Palaeolithic blade-and-burin industry), Gudimallam
  [3:0:8.4] Anantapur | Guntakal (bone implements), Lattavaram, Velpulamadugu
  [3:0:8.5] Nellore, Kadapa | Pennar valley (Foote: 28 quartzite tools), Sagileru
[3:0:9] - **Kurnool** is the Palaeolithic area of AP (Bagor = Rajasthan, Sarai Nahar Rai and Belan valley = UP).
[3:0:10] - **Nagarjunakonda:** Lower Palaeolithic tools in river gravels studied by K. V. Soundara Rajan, which record **alternating dry and wet climatic phases** (APPSC key: three cycles); also microliths and an Iron Age–Megalithic centre.
[3:0:11] - Mar 2026: Upper Palaeolithic **quartz tools** and **petroglyphs** at **Manchirevula** near Hyderabad.
[3:0:12] # Tools by stage and raw material
[3:0:13] TABLE head: Stage | Tools | Raw material
  [3:0:13.0] Lower Palaeolithic | Handaxe, cleaver, chopper | Quartzite (Kadapa–Kurnool); limestone north of the Krishna
  [3:0:13.1] Middle Palaeolithic | Flakes, scrapers, borers, points | Chert, jasper, chalcedony
  [3:0:13.2] Upper Palaeolithic | Blades, burins, bone tools | Silica stone, bone
  [3:0:13.3] Mesolithic | Microliths | Chert, jasper; weapons of dolomite
[3:0:14] ~~ Sources: NextGen AP HISTORY Part 1 p.2-3; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.48-49; K. V. Soundara Rajan, Ancient India 14 (1958); app notes 1:3
## SUB 1 | Megalithic (Iron Age) Andhra – Rakshasa Gullu and Burial Types
[3:1:0] - Andhra megaliths = **Rakshasa Gullu** ("large stones"), of the **Iron Age**, with **black-and-red ware** (absent only at **Visakhapatnam**).
[3:1:1] - Local names: **Pandava Gullu** (Kurnool, Kadapa), **Kurambara Gullu** (Chittoor), **Morivara Gullu** (Anantapur).
[3:1:2] - About **12 types** in AP (studied by **N. R. Banerji**); grave goods: pottery, beads, **iron implements**, gold ornaments – not glass mirrors; **dolmens for elites, pits for commoners**.
[3:1:3] - Sites: **Nagarjunakonda** (main habitation centre), Brahmagiri, Gudivada, **Jonnawada** (Nellore), Boodigapalli, Naidupalli; a coffin with a male and a female body in one grave near **Yeleswaram**.
[3:1:4] - Jun 2026: **Mayiladumparai** (TN) AMS dating puts the South Indian Iron Age at c. **2100 BCE** (2172 BCE), earlier than the Gangetic Iron Age.
[3:1:5] - H. D. Sankalia: **rice** was first harvested and eaten in Andhra in this **Iron/Megalithic** age.
[3:1:6] TABLE head: Type | Form | AP area
  [3:1:6.0] Cist | Stone-slab box | NextGen: found everywhere except Visakhapatnam (the G2 key treated "many in Visakhapatnam" as correct)
  [3:1:6.1] Dolmen | Table-like chamber of slabs | Krishna, Guntur, East and West Godavari; also Chittoor
  [3:1:6.2] Sarcophagus | Terracotta coffin | Sankhavaram (Kurnool) – ram-shaped; Nagarjunakonda; NOT a Chittoor–Anantapuramu cluster
  [3:1:6.3] Menhir | Upright stone | Revarala (Krishna)
  [3:1:6.4] Anthropomorphic | Human-shaped stone | Tirupati – India's largest collection
[3:1:7] > G2 PYQ: the incorrect statement (official key) = "sarcophagus burials are more in Chittoor and Anantapuramu".
[3:1:8] - **Warangal** region: famous for **iron implements** and steel (wootz) – the iron tradition of the Telangana–Andhra region.
[3:1:9] ~~ Sources: NextGen AP HISTORY Part 1 p.5; NextGen Powerplay #1 Part 1.1 p.3-4; inflibnet e-PG "Megaliths of South India" (Sankavaram); app notes 1:3 (sub 6)
## SUB 2 | Neolithic–Chalcolithic Andhra – Patapadu, Keesarapalli, Utnur
[3:2:0] TABLE head: Site | Location | Feature
  [3:2:0.0] Patapadu | Kurnool | Chalcolithic – painted pottery (Copper Age evidence of Andhra)
  [3:2:0.1] Keesarapalli | Krishna | Chalcolithic – copper tools
  [3:2:0.2] Utnur | Mahbubnagar, Telangana | Neolithic ash mound ("mounds of burning soot")
  [3:2:0.3] Palavoy | Anantapur | Neolithic settlement, four ash mounds
  [3:2:0.4] Veerapuram | Kurnool, right bank of the Krishna | Neolithic–Chalcolithic to Early Historic sequence
  [3:2:0.5] Ramapuram | Kurnool | Neolithic–Chalcolithic
  [3:2:0.6] Sanganakallu, Tekkalakota | Ballari, Karnataka | Neolithic settlements – not Andhra
  [3:2:0.7] Brahmagiri | Chitradurga, Karnataka | Copper objects; megalithic graves
[3:2:1] - First Neolithic tool in South India: **Meadows Taylor, 1842**, at **Lingasugur** (Raichur) – not Jwalapuram.
[3:2:2] - Neolithic weapons of AP mostly of **dolomite**; sandstone axes near **Vaddamanu** (Amaravati).
[3:2:3] ~~ Sources: NextGen AP HISTORY Part 1 p.4-5; app notes 1:3 (sub 4)
[3:2:4] - **Disposal of the dead (Neolithic–Chalcolithic Andhra):** adults in **extended burials**, often with pots; children in urns – not cremation or mound burials.
[3:2:5] - **Utnur ash mounds:** formed by repeatedly **burning cow dung inside wooden cattle stockades** rebuilt many times (Allchin) → proof of Neolithic **animal domestication**.
## SUB 3 | Mesolithic Andhra – Giddaluru and the Microlithic Centres
[3:3:0] - **Giddaluru (Prakasam)** = the **microlithic implement centre** of Andhra identified by archaeologists (Nellore, Kurnool, Guntur are distractors).
[3:3:1] - Microliths also at **Nagarjunakonda** and **Kondapur**; tool centres discovered by **Robert Bruce Foote**.
[3:3:2] - Mesolithic = **microlithic**; tools by **flaking and chipping**; Andhra microliths resemble those of **South India and the Caspian** region.
[3:3:3] - Microlithic implements of c. 5000 BCE at **Giddaluru and Khammam**.
[3:3:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen AP HISTORY Part 1 p.3-4
[3:3:5] - **Raw material:** South Indian Mesolithic microliths were mostly of **milky quartz**; found at **Nagarjunakonda** and **Renigunta**, among others.
## SUB 4 | Rock Art and Caves of Andhra
[3:4:0] - **Borra Caves** (Ananthagiri hills, Araku, **Visakhapatnam**): karstic limestone caves; discovered **1807** by William King (GSI); Mesolithic example.
[3:4:1] - **Ketavaram** (Kurnool): Mesolithic rock paintings; most painted animal in AP rock art = **deer**.
[3:4:2] - Neolithic paintings: **Chintagunta** (man with bow), **Velpulamadugu** (humped bulls facing each other), **Thenagala** (dog and deer in red) – Anantapur.
[3:4:3] - **Petroglyphs** are **carved**, not painted.
[3:4:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen AP HISTORY Part 1 p.4-5

=== NOTES 1:4 | Pre-harappan and Early Harappan Cultures – Mehrgarh, Amri, Kot Diji, Kalibangan-i
## SUB 0 | Mehrgarh – Earliest Farming and Settled Life
[4:0:0] - **Mehrgarh** (Kachhi plain, Baluchistan, near the **Bolan Pass**; c. **7000 BCE**): **earliest agriculture** (wheat, barley), domestication and **settled life** in the subcontinent; excavated by **J.-F. Jarrige** (1974).
[4:0:1] - Seven periods (I–VII) give remains **from the Stone Age (Neolithic) to the Harappan** period – unlike Amri, Kot Diji or Kalibangan.
[4:0:2] TABLE head: Period | Evidence
  [4:0:2.0] I (c. 7000 BCE) | Aceramic Neolithic; mud-brick houses; barley, wheat; sheep, goat, cattle
  [4:0:2.1] II | Pottery and cotton (c. 5000 BCE, oldest cotton) begin
  [4:0:2.2] III onward | Copper working, wheel-made painted pottery, beads
[4:0:3] - **Burials:** under or near the houses, flexed, with grave goods, sometimes with **goats**.
[4:0:4] - Neolithic Baluchistan and Indus plains: crops **wheat, barley, dates, cotton**; main animals **sheep and goats**.
[4:0:5] TABLE head: Early village | Area | Excavator
  [4:0:5.0] Kili Gul Muhammad | Quetta valley | W. A. Fairservis
  [4:0:5.1] Damb Sadaat | Quetta valley (Early Harappan phase) | W. A. Fairservis
  [4:0:5.2] Rana Ghundai | Zhob-Loralai valley | E. J. Ross
[4:0:6] > Mehrgarh is a **Neolithic** site, not a Harappan city; "earliest settled life" and "earliest agriculture" = Mehrgarh (not Kili Gul Muhammad, Quetta or Kalat).
[4:0:7] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.53-56; NextGen Indian History Part 1 p.1; app notes 1:4
## SUB 1 | Early Harappan Cultures – Amri, Kot Diji, Sothi, Kalibangan-I
[4:1:0] - Early (Pre-) Harappan phase **3300–2600 BCE** = **Regionalisation Era** (Hakra phase); copper, the wheel and the **plough** were already known.
[4:1:1] TABLE head: Culture / site | Area | Feature | Excavator
  [4:1:1.0] Amri | Sindh | Transitional pre- to Mature culture; rhinoceros remains; later Jhangar | N. G. Majumdar (1929), later J.-M. Casal
  [4:1:1.1] Kot Diji | Sindh | Fortified, destroyed by fire; many stone implements | Ghurye; F. A. Khan (1955)
  [4:1:1.2] Sothi-Siswal | Rajasthan–Haryana (Ghaggar) | Pre-Harappan pottery | A. Ghosh identified Sothi
  [4:1:1.3] Hakra | Cholistan (Ghaggar-Hakra) – not Gujarat | – | –
  [4:1:1.4] Banawali | Fatehabad, Haryana | Pre- and Mature phases | R. S. Bisht (1974)
  [4:1:1.5] Mitathal | Bhiwani, Haryana | Pre-, Mature and Late phases | Suraj Bhan (1968)
[4:1:2] - **Amalananda Ghosh** (1953) was the **first to see the likeness between a pre-Harappan culture (Sothi) and the Mature Harappan**.
[4:1:3] - Pottery was regional (Amri, Kot Diji, Sothi-Siswal, Hakra wares), not uniform; Early Harappans used the potter's wheel.
[4:1:4] # Kalibangan-I
[4:1:5] - **Earliest ploughed field** with **criss-cross furrows** = **mixed cropping**; mud-brick fortification; pottery in **six fabrics**.
[4:1:6] - Excavated **1961–69** by **B. B. Lal and B. K. Thapar**. The ploughed field is pre-Harappan; the fire altars belong to the Mature phase.
[4:1:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History Part 1 p.1, p.13-15; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.72-73; Wikipedia "Amalananda Ghosh"; app notes 1:4
## SUB 2 | Origin Debate and Excavators
[4:2:0] - **W. A. Fairservis** wrote "**The Roots of Ancient India**" (1971) on the prehistoric village cultures of Baluchistan.
[4:2:1] TABLE head: View on Harappan origin | Scholar
  [4:2:1.0] Indigenous growth | John Marshall
  [4:2:1.1] Migration from Sumer | E. J. H. Mackay
  [4:2:1.2] Diffusion of ideas from West Asia | Mortimer Wheeler
  [4:2:1.3] Local evolution from the Mehrgarh Neolithic | Current consensus
[4:2:2] - Kalibangan and Kot Diji foreshadow Mature buildings and pottery → urbanisation was **gradual and local**, not imported from Mesopotamia.
[4:2:3] - Unsolved: the **gap between Neolithic Mehrgarh and the urban phase**.
[4:2:4] - 23 Jun 2026: **Rakhigarhi** (~550 ha) shows unbroken Early to Mature occupation; Haryana protected **Tighrana and Mitathal** (**Bhiwani**).
[4:2:5] ~~ Sources: Wikipedia "Walter Fairservis"; app notes 1:4 (sub 4-5)

[n5b31eb91320] sub2
  Which issue remains an open problem in the study of Harappan origins?

  x: The gap between Neolithic Mehrgarh and the later urban phase remains an open problem.
[n7f4943ead5c] sub2
  Who suggested that the Harappan Civilisation arose through migration from Sumer?
  John Marshall | Mortimer Wheeler | *E. J. H. Mackay | Rakhal Das Banerji
  x: Mackay suggested migration from Sumer; Marshall proposed indigenous evolution and Wheeler diffusion from West Asia.
[f9f874fda2650] sub2
  Who held that the Harappan Civilisation arose by diffusion of ideas from West Asia?
  John Marshall | E. J. H. Mackay | *Mortimer Wheeler | W. A. Fairservis
  x: Wheeler proposed West Asian diffusion; Mackay proposed migration from Sumer and Marshall indigenous evolution.
[n6bccd740b2e] sub2
  Which development is regarded as evidence that the transition from the Early to the Mature Harappan phase was gradual and internally rooted?
  *The anticipation of Mature Harappan pottery and buildings at Kalibangan and Kot Diji | The complete disappearance of fortified settlements | The abandonment of agriculture before urbanisation | The sudden replacement of all local crafts by Mesopotamian goods
  x: Pottery and buildings at Kalibangan and Kot Diji anticipate Mature Harappan forms, supporting gradual and internal urbanisation.
[nc09ad348374] sub2
  Which conclusion is most consistent with the significance of Mehrgarh and the Early Harappan phase?
  The civilisation originated exclusively in Mesopotamia and was later copied locally. | *The civilisation developed locally rather than being entirely imported from Mesopotamia. | The Early Harappan phase was unrelated to the Mature Harappan civilisation. | The civilisation had no agricultural or village stage before urbanisation.
  x: Evidence from Mehrgarh and Early Harappan sites supports local growth and regional evolution, rather than an exclusively Mesopotamian origin.
[n2c86bc514f0] sub2
  Rakhigarhi, about 550 hectares in extent, shows continuous occupation across which phases?

  x: Rakhigarhi covers approximately 550 hectares and shows continuous occupation from the Early to the Mature Harappan phase.

[n16c878bbe70] sub2

  Indigenous evolution — Mackay | *Migration from Sumer — Mackay | Diffusion of ideas — John Marshall | Regional growth from Mesopotamia — Wheeler
  x: Mackay supported the migration-from-Sumer theory. Marshall was associated with indigenous evolution, while Wheeler was associated with diffusion of ideas.
[n939c8841ae3] sub2
  What does the current consensus on the origin of the Harappan Civilisation emphasise?
  *Regional growth from the Mehrgarh tradition | A sudden origin through Greek colonisation | Exclusive development in the Ghaggar-Hakra valley without earlier roots | A complete replacement of local communities by Sumerian migrants
  x: The current consensus emphasises indigenous regional development from the Mehrgarh tradition, rather than complete external replacement.

=== NOTES 1:5 | Indus Valley Civilization – Discovery, Chronology, Geographical Extent, Boundary Sites
## SUB 0 | Site → State, River and Country
[5:0:0] # Sites by present state / country
[5:0:1] TABLE head: Country / state | Sites
  [5:0:1.0] Pakistan – Sindh | Mohenjodaro (Larkana, right bank of the Indus), Chanhudaro (Indus, ~130 km south of Mohenjodaro), Kot Diji, Amri
  [5:0:1.1] Pakistan – Punjab | Harappa (Sahiwal/Montgomery, left bank of the Ravi)
  [5:0:1.2] Pakistan – Baluchistan | Sutkagendor (Makran coast), Balakot (Kot Bala), Dabarkot; Mehrgarh (Neolithic)
  [5:0:1.3] Gujarat | Lothal (Saragwala, Dholka, Ahmedabad), Dholavira and Surkotada (both Kutch), Desalpur, Rangpur (Saurashtra), Rojdi, Kuntasi, Padri
  [5:0:1.4] Rajasthan | Kalibangan (Hanumangarh, Ghaggar)
  [5:0:1.5] Haryana | Rakhigarhi (Hisar), Banawali (Fatehabad), Mitathal, Balu, Bhagwanpura (Kurukshetra)
  [5:0:1.6] Punjab (India) | Ropar (Sutlej)
  [5:0:1.7] Uttar Pradesh | Alamgirpur (Meerut), Mandi (Muzaffarnagar), Hulas (Saharanpur), Sinauli (Baghpat)
  [5:0:1.8] Maharashtra | Daimabad (Ahmednagar, Pravara)
  [5:0:1.9] J&K | Manda (Chenab)
[5:0:2] - **Indian site** among Harappa, Mohenjodaro, Kot Bala, Mehrgarh = **Lothal**; Pakistani site among Kalibangan, Lothal, Alamgirpur = **Harappa**.
[5:0:3] - **Wrong pair to spot:** Rakhigarhi – **Rajasthan** (it is Haryana).
[5:0:4] - **Sindh:** Mohenjodaro and Chanhudaro (Harappa = Punjab, Surkotada = Gujarat).
[5:0:5] # Site → river
[5:0:6] TABLE head: Site | River
  [5:0:6.0] Harappa | Ravi
  [5:0:6.1] Mohenjodaro, Chanhudaro | Indus
  [5:0:6.2] Lothal | Bhogava (near the Sabarmati confluence) – not the Narmada
  [5:0:6.3] Kalibangan | Ghaggar
  [5:0:6.4] Banawali | Rangoi (Ghaggar valley)
  [5:0:6.5] Ropar | Sutlej
  [5:0:6.6] Rangpur | Bhadar
  [5:0:6.7] Alamgirpur | Hindon
  [5:0:6.8] Daimabad | Pravara
  [5:0:6.9] Manda | Chenab
[5:0:7] - **Ghaggar valley** site among Alamgirpur, Lothal, Mohenjodaro, Banawali = **Banawali**.
[5:0:8] - **Mandi** (Muzaffarnagar): Late Harappan **jewellery hoard**, June 2000. **Hulas** (Saharanpur): Late Harappan; chert blades, bone points. **Sinauli** (Baghpat): **125 graves**, north–south.
[5:0:9] - Dholavira lies on **Khadir** island (Kutch).
[5:0:10] ~~ Sources: NextGen Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.19-26; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.79-83; app notes 1:5 (sub 5)
## SUB 1 | Basic Facts – Bronze Age, Area, Rivers, Non-Aryan Character
[5:1:0] - **Proto-historic** (script undeciphered), **Bronze Age**; India's **first urbanisation**.
[5:1:1] - Area about **1.3 million sq km** – larger than Egypt and Mesopotamia; population ~5 million; matured in **Sindh and Punjab** and spread **south and east**.
[5:1:2] - Highest concentration of sites on the **Ghaggar–Hakra** system (about 250 sites, 6 cities).
[5:1:3] - **Rivers:** Sindhu, **Chenab, Jhelum**, Ravi, Sutlej, Ghaggar – up to the **Hindon** (Alamgirpur); **not the Ganga**.
[5:1:4] - **Main sites:** Mohenjodaro, Harappa, Ropar, Kalibangan; roads, houses and drains were planned.
[5:1:5] - **Metals:** copper, bronze, gold, **silver** (first use of silver in India), lead, tin – but **no iron**.
[5:1:6] - **Non-Aryan:** Harappan culture was **urban**; the Rigvedic Aryans were **pastoral/rural**.
[5:1:7] TABLE head: Period | Economy
  [5:1:7.0] Indus Valley | Urban
  [5:1:7.1] Rigvedic | Pastoral
  [5:1:7.2] Later Vedic | Agrarian
[5:1:8] - **Not Harappan:** **Sohgaura** (Gorakhpur – Mauryan copper plate), **Pataliputra** (Magadha capital), Mehrgarh (Neolithic), Burzahom (Neolithic pit dwellings).
[5:1:9] - Assertion–reason: Mohenjodaro and Harappa are "dead" cities **and** were found by excavation – both true, but R does not explain A.
[5:1:10] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History Part 1 p.4-5, p.7; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.26-27, p.79; app notes 1:5 (sub 0, 3, 6)
## SUB 2 | Names, Meluhha and Contemporary Civilisations
[5:2:0] TABLE head: Name | Meaning
  [5:2:0.0] Meluhha | Mesopotamian name for the Indus region
  [5:2:0.1] Dilmun | Bahrain (intermediate station)
  [5:2:0.2] Magan (Makan) | Makran coast / Oman
  [5:2:0.3] Mohenjodaro | "Mound of the Dead" (Sindhi)
  [5:2:0.4] Lothal | "Mini Harappa" (not City of the Dead)
  [5:2:0.5] Chanhudaro | "Lancashire of India"
  [5:2:0.6] Kalibangan | "Black bangles"
  [5:2:0.7] Dholavira | Local name Kotada
[5:2:1] - **Harappan Civilisation** is preferred: named by Marshall after **Harappa**, the first site found; the culture went beyond the Indus valley. "Sindhu-Sarasvati" stresses the Ghaggar-Hakra sites.
[5:2:2] - **Bronze Age river civilisations:** Mesopotamia – Tigris and Euphrates; **Egypt – Nile**; China – Hwang Ho; Harappa – Indus.
[5:2:3] - **First proper writing:** **Sumerian cuneiform** (Mesopotamia); Egypt – hieroglyphics; the Indus script is undeciphered.
[5:2:4] - **Makli Necropolis** (Thatta, Sindh) – one of the world's largest necropolises (UNESCO) – is not Mohenjodaro.
[5:2:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History Part 1 p.2-4; UNESCO WHC (Makli); app notes 1:5 (sub 1)
## SUB 3 | Discovery and Excavators
[5:3:0] - **1826**: **Charles Masson** described the Harappa ruins (1826-38). **1853, 1856**: **Alexander Cunningham** saw pottery, tools and a seal but missed their value.
[5:3:1] - **1857**: Harappan bricks used as ballast for the **Karachi–Lahore** railway. **1912**: **J. Fleet** published Harappan seals.
[5:3:2] - **Sir John Marshall** (DG, ASI 1902–28) directed the 1921-22 campaign; announced the civilisation in 1924; coined "Indus Valley Civilisation".
[5:3:3] - The two Indian discoverers: **Daya Ram Sahni** and **Rakhaldas Banerji**.
[5:3:4] TABLE head: Site | Discoverer / excavator | Year
  [5:3:4.0] Harappa (first site) | Daya Ram Sahni; later M. S. Vats (1926-34), Wheeler | 1921
  [5:3:4.1] Mohenjodaro | R. D. Banerji; later Marshall, K. N. Dikshit, Mackay | 1922
  [5:3:4.2] Chanhudaro | Found by N. G. Majumdar (1931); excavated by E. J. H. Mackay (1935-36) | 1931
  [5:3:4.3] Lothal | S. R. Rao | 1954
  [5:3:4.4] Rangpur | M. S. Vats (1931); S. R. Rao | –
  [5:3:4.5] Ropar | Found by B. B. Lal (1950); dug by Y. D. Sharma (1953-55); first site dug in independent India | 1950
  [5:3:4.6] Daimabad | B. P. Bopardikar | 1958
  [5:3:4.7] Kalibangan | A. Ghosh (1953); B. B. Lal and B. K. Thapar (1961-69) | –
  [5:3:4.8] Surkotada | Jagatpati (J. P.) Joshi | 1964
  [5:3:4.9] Dholavira | Found by J. P. Joshi (1967-68); excavated by R. S. Bisht from 1990 | –
  [5:3:4.10] Banawali | R. S. Bisht | 1974
  [5:3:4.11] Manda | J. P. Joshi and Madhubala | 1982
  [5:3:4.12] Rakhigarhi | Amarendra Nath | 1997
[5:3:5] - Order of discovery: **Harappa (1921) → Mohenjodaro (1922) → Chanhudaro (1931) → Lothal (1954)**.
[5:3:6] - **V. A. Smith** (historian) was **not** linked with the Harappa–Mohenjodaro digs. Macaulay, Clive and Col. Tod are distractors for Marshall.
[5:3:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History Part 1 p.1, p.4; NextGen Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.17; app notes 1:5 (sub 2)
## SUB 4 | Largest Sites, UNESCO and Recent Developments
[5:4:0] - **Rakhigarhi** (Hisar, Haryana; ~550 ha) = **largest Harappan site in India**; **Dholavira** second; **Mohenjodaro** largest in Pakistan. Five largest: Harappa, Mohenjodaro, Ganweriwala, Rakhigarhi, Dholavira.
[5:4:1] - **Dholavira:** UNESCO WHS **2021** – India's **40th**, Gujarat's 4th, the **first IVC site in India**; absence of encroachment helped.
[5:4:2] - **Mohenjodaro:** UNESCO WHS since **1980**. **Rakhigarhi:** on the UNESCO **Tentative List** (Jun 2026).
[5:4:3] - **Union Budget 2020-21:** **five** iconic archaeological sites – **Rakhigarhi, Hastinapur, Shivsagar, Dholavira, Adichanallur**.
[5:4:4] - Jun 2026: Haryana protected **Tighrana and Mitathal** (Bhiwani) under the **Haryana Ancient and Historical Monuments Act, 1964**.
[5:4:5] - 23 Jun 2026: ASI transferred Rakhigarhi's **56 skeletons** to the **Anthropological Survey of India**; a 4,500-year-old skeleton gave the ancient DNA study.
[5:4:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History Part 1 p.16-17; NextGen Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.22; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.80; app notes 1:5 (sub 8)
## SUB 5 | Boundary Sites
[5:5:0] TABLE head: Direction | Site | Place / river
  [5:5:0.0] North | Manda | Chenab, Jammu; base for Himalayan wood
  [5:5:0.1] South | Daimabad | Pravara, Ahmednagar, Maharashtra
  [5:5:0.2] East | Alamgirpur | Hindon, Meerut, western UP
  [5:5:0.3] West | Sutkagendor | Makran coast, Baluchistan
[5:5:1] - N–S–E–W = **Manda – Daimabad – Alamgirpur – Sutkagendor**; Alamgirpur finds reflect **Harappan** culture (Late Harappan, cloth impression).
[5:5:2] - **Shortughai** (Afghanistan): trade outpost for **lapis lazuli**.
[5:5:3] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History Part 1 p.15, p.17; NextGen Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.24-26
## SUB 6 | Chronology and Phases
[5:6:0] TABLE head: Phase | Dates | Features
  [5:6:0.0] Early Harappan | 3300–2600 BCE | Kot Diji, Amri, Sothi; villages
  [5:6:0.1] Mature Harappan | 2600–1900 BCE | Planned cities, 1:2:4 bricks, script
  [5:6:0.2] Late Harappan | 1900–1300 BCE | De-urbanisation; OCP, Cemetery-H
[5:6:1] - **John Marshall:** 3250–2750 BCE by **Mesopotamian cross-dating**; **D. P. Agrawal** (radiocarbon): **2500–1750 BCE**.
[5:6:2] - **Dholavira:** inhabited c. **3000–1800 BCE** in **seven stages** (R. S. Bisht); UNESCO: 3rd to mid-2nd millennium BCE.
[5:6:3] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History Part 1 p.1-2, p.17; app notes 1:5 (sub 3)

=== NOTES 1:6 | Ivc Town Planning – Grid Layout, Citadel and Lower Town, Drainage, Great Bath, Granaries
## SUB 0 | Hallmarks of the IVC – Town Planning, Burnt Bricks, Bronze Age
[6:0:0] # What the exams ask
[6:0:1] - **Hallmark / specialisation:** **town planning** (urban centres, grid, drains) is the single most distinctive feature of the IVC (APPSC G2 2011) – not "all of these".
[6:0:2] - **Most significant feature:** **burnt-brick buildings** – large-scale use of burnt bricks in almost all constructions (BPSC 2018, JPSC 2023). Egypt used mainly dried bricks; no "first true arches", no buildings of worship.
[6:0:3] - **Known for (UK UDA 2003):** town planning, Mohenjodaro and Harappa, agriculture and craft industry (bead, shell, metal factories) → "all of these".
[6:0:4] - **No temples or palaces** clearly found, unlike Mesopotamia and Egypt.
[6:0:5] - Standard bricks, grid, streets and drains point to a **civic authority**; the absence of palaces or temples does **not** disprove it.
[6:0:6] - **Not known:** the **true arch** (only corbelled), and **iron** – IVC is a **Bronze Age** culture; iron came c. 1000 BC (Later Vedic). Pillars (Mohenjodaro assembly hall, 20 pillars), wells, drains and bows-and-arrows were all known.
[6:0:7] # IVC vs Vedic / Aryan
[6:0:8] TABLE head: Point | IVC (Harappan) | Vedic
  [6:0:8.0] Life | Urban, planned cities | Rural, pastoral
  [6:0:8.1] Script | Pictographic (undeciphered) | No script
  [6:0:8.2] Metal | Copper, bronze; no iron | Iron in Later Vedic age
  [6:0:8.3] Pottery | Red ware painted in black | Painted Grey Ware
[6:0:9] - **Why IVC is placed before the Aryans (UPPCS 1990):** **pottery** – the stratified pottery sequence (from Mehrgarh to the black-on-red Harappan ware) lies below the Painted Grey Ware of the Vedic people.
[6:0:10] - **IVC different from Vedic (UPPCS 2004):** city amenities + pictographic script + no iron → "all of the above".
[6:0:11] > Trap: "the IVC knew iron" is always the false statement.
[6:0:12] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.83-86, p.101, p.107; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.5, p.7; old app notes row 6
## SUB 1 | Wells, Drainage and Water Management
[6:1:0] TABLE head: Site | Feature
  [6:1:0.0] Mohenjodaro | About 700 brick wells, many inside houses → most wells (UPPCS 2004)
  [6:1:0.1] Dholavira | Water management: 16 reservoirs, dams, embankments, channels; some rock-cut (UPSC 2021, CDS 2012, UPPCS 2010)
  [6:1:0.2] Kalibangan | Many houses had their own wells; wooden drains
  [6:1:0.3] Banawali | No systematic drainage
  [6:1:0.4] Allahdino (Sindh) | Well irrigation
[6:1:1] - **Drainage** = the most advanced IVC feature: house drains → sump/cesspit → **covered** street drains (bricks or stone slabs) with **manholes** → **soak pits** or main channels; streets sloped for flow; better than contemporary Mesopotamia and Egypt. Lothal: mud-brick houses but burnt-brick drains.
[6:1:2] - **Dholavira reservoirs:** water from the **Manhar and Mansar** rivulets; huge rock-cut reservoir about **73–79 m long, 7 m deep**; stepwells cut in sandstone = Harappan **rock-cut architecture** (UPPSC 2006).
[6:1:3] > Trap: Dholavira = reservoirs (water harvesting); Mohenjodaro = wells.
[6:1:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.85-86, p.102-103; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.14; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.21-22; old app notes row 6
## SUB 2 | Citadel and Lower Town – Dholavira's Three Parts
[6:2:0] TABLE head: Site | Plan
  [6:2:0.0] Mohenjodaro, Harappa | Citadel on the west (smaller, higher, on a mud-brick platform, elite and public buildings) + lower town on the east (larger, commoners)
  [6:2:0.1] Kalibangan | Citadel + lower town, both fortified
  [6:2:0.2] Dholavira | Only city in three parts: citadel, middle town, lower town
  [6:2:0.3] Chanhudaro | No citadel (only Harappan city without one)
  [6:2:0.4] Lothal, Surkotada | Citadel not separately walled; inside one fortified enclosure
  [6:2:0.5] Sutkagendor | Citadel + lower city
[6:2:1] - **Great Bath, granary, college of priests, assembly hall** stood on the **citadel** of Mohenjodaro → both "citadel on the west" and "Great Bath in the citadel" are correct (AP Sachivalayam).
[6:2:2] - Pataliputra is NOT an IVC site; Kalibangan, Ropar and Lothal are.
[6:2:3] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.84-85, p.102; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.5, p.14; old app notes row 6
## SUB 3 | Bricks, Streets and Houses
[6:3:0] # Bricks
[6:3:1] - **Burnt bricks** common; **mud bricks** for fillings and platforms and at **Kalibangan**. Both baked and raw bricks used.
[6:3:2] - Standard ratio **1:2:4** (thickness : width : length) = 4:2:1.
[6:3:3] - **Same ratio, different sizes:** house bricks **7.5×15×30 cm** (≈7×14×28), city-wall bricks **10×20×40 cm** → "the same size of brick for all constructions" is **false**.
[6:3:4] - **Dholavira**: **sandstone and limestone** blocks, not baked brick.
[6:3:5] - **Ornamental bricks** = Kalibangan; **curved bricks** = Chanhudaro.
[6:3:6] # Streets
[6:3:7] - **Grid layout**: streets ran north-south and east-west, crossing at **right angles**, splitting the city into blocks → **broad and straight** (not narrow, zig-zag).
[6:3:8] - Main streets about **10 m (30 feet)** wide (Rocket: main road of Mohenjodaro ≈9.15 m). **Banawali** = only city with **radial streets**.
[6:3:9] # Houses
[6:3:10] - Rooms round a **central courtyard**; brick-paved **bathrooms** and toilets; no ground-level windows (privacy).
[6:3:11] - Doors and windows opened on **side lanes**, not main streets (Lothal is the exception – entrances on the main street).
[6:3:12] - **Storeys:** most houses single-storeyed but **staircases** show many had an upper storey. CGPSC 2022 key treats "mostly all houses single storey" as untrue along with "same size of bricks" → **(i) and (ii)**; "main roads ~10 m" is true.
[6:3:13] - **Arch:** Harappans used only the corbelled arch, never the true arch (MPPSC 2009).
[6:3:14] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.85-86; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.5; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.19; old app notes row 6
## SUB 4 | The Great Bath
[6:4:0] TABLE head: Angle | Fact
  [6:4:0.0] Site | Mohenjodaro, on the citadel mound, Sindh – not Harappa, Lothal, Ropar or Kalibangan
  [6:4:0.1] Size | 11.88 × 7.01 m, 2.43 m deep; two flights of steps on north and south
  [6:4:0.2] Material | Burnt bricks set in gypsum mortar; bitumen (natural tar) waterproofing
  [6:4:0.3] Water | From a large well in a side room; drain in one corner
  [6:4:0.4] Purpose | Ritual bath (most view); public bath ruled out as houses had bathrooms
[6:4:1] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.84; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.10, p.12; old app notes row 6
## SUB 5 | Granaries and Other Public Buildings
[6:5:0] TABLE head: Structure | Site
  [6:5:0.0] Great Granary – largest building (45.71 × 15.23 m), on the citadel | Mohenjodaro (Hostel Welfare 2017; APPSC G2 2008)
  [6:5:0.1] Granary of 12 rooms in two rows of six, near the Ravi | Harappa
  [6:5:0.2] Granaries also at | Kalibangan, Rakhigarhi
  [6:5:0.3] Warehouse on a raised platform beside the dock, seal impressions | Lothal
  [6:5:0.4] Pillared assembly hall (20 pillars in 4 rows) | Mohenjodaro
  [6:5:0.5] College of priests | Mohenjodaro
  [6:5:0.6] Fire altars | Kalibangan (seven), Lothal, Banawali, Rakhigarhi
[6:5:1] - Granaries stored the farm surplus (cereals probably taken as tax).
[6:5:2] - **Harappa finds:** Cemetery H, R-37, coffin burial, wheat and barley in a wooden mortar, dice, unicorn seal. Not Harappa: dockyard = Lothal; horse bones = Surkotada; bronze chariot = Daimabad.
[6:5:3] > Trap: Great Granary = Mohenjodaro; two rows of six = Harappa.
[6:5:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.84-85; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.11-12; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.19; old app notes row 6
## SUB 6 | Dholavira – Plan, Stadium and Signboard
[6:6:0] - **Kachchh** (Khadir island), Gujarat; rectangular plan; one of the **five largest** cities (with Mohenjodaro, Harappa, Ganweriwala, Rakhigarhi).
[6:6:1] - **Stadium** (~283 × 47 m) between citadel and middle town; special cemetery structures; **ten-sign signboard** near the **North Gate** = largest Harappan signs.
[6:6:2] - Settled in seven stages, **c. 3000–1500 BCE** (APPSC 2018 marked 3000–1800 BCE).
[6:6:3] - **UNESCO 2021**: India's **40th** WHS; **first IVC site in India** on the list.
[6:6:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.102; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.14, p.16; old app notes row 6

=== OWN MCQs of 1:6 (67)
[nee857822326] sub0
  What do the standardised bricks, grid planning and drainage of the Indus Valley Civilisation suggest?
  *They suggest civic authority and early engineering skill. | They prove that all settlements were royal capitals. | They show that the civilization had abandoned urban planning. | They indicate the exclusive control of religious institutions.
  x: Standardisation of bricks, grid planning and drainage points to civic authority and considerable early engineering skill.
[ndd9dfd80dd8] sub0
  Which of the following is NOT correct about Indus Valley cities?
  No palaces or temples have been clearly identified in them. | Their drainage was more advanced than that of contemporary Mesopotamia and Egypt. | *The absence of palaces or temples proves there was no civic authority. | Their standardised urban planning suggests a civic authority.
  x: The absence of clearly identified palaces or temples does not disprove civic authority; standardised planning instead suggests it.
[f43198e08d003] sub0
  Which of the following has NOT been clearly found in Harappan cities, unlike Mesopotamia and Egypt?
  Granaries | *Palaces | Covered drains | Wells
  x: No temples or palaces have been clearly found in IVC cities, though granaries, covered drains and wells exist.
[fcdfa6ea32532] sub0
  The Indus Valley Civilization belongs to which age?
  Iron Age | Neolithic Age | Palaeolithic Age | *Bronze Age
  x: The IVC was a Bronze Age culture; iron came much later, around 1000 BC.
[gfe48b7e52ead] sub0
  Which of the following is regarded as the hallmark of the Indus Valley Civilisation?
  Temple architecture | *Town planning | Iron technology | Coinage
  x: Planned towns with grid streets, standard bricks and covered drains are the hallmark of the IVC; temples, iron and coins are not found.
[g7d53af50800b] sub0

2. Clear remains of temples and palaces have been found in Harappan cities.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  *1 only | 2 only | Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: Town planning is the hallmark of the IVC, but no temples or palaces have been clearly found, unlike in Mesopotamia and Egypt.

[fb3a603e0788f] sub5

  x: Kalibangan yielded seven fire altars; fire altars are also known from Lothal.

  Mohenjodaro | Lothal | Kalibangan | *Harappa
  x: Harappa had a granary of 12 rooms in two rows of six near the river; the Great Granary on the citadel was at Mohenjodaro.

  Horse bones – Surkotada | Bronze chariot – Daimabad | *Dockyard – Harappa | Cemetery H – Harappa
  x: The dockyard is at Lothal, not Harappa; horse bones are from Surkotada and the bronze chariot from Daimabad.

  x: Wheat and barley in a wooden mortar are among the finds at Harappa.

  6 | 10 | *12 | 16
  x: The Harappa granary had two rows of six rooms each, giving 12 rooms.

  x: Defective: all four pairs are correct. Besides Kalibangan and Lothal, fire altars were also found at Banawali and Rakhigarhi (PMF), so "Fire altars – Banawali" is not a wrong pair; signboard–Dholavira, horse remains–Surkotada and rice husk–Lothal are also right.
