=== NOTES 1:7 | Major Harappan Sites and Their Signature Finds
## SUB 0 | Signature Find → Site
[7:0:0] TABLE head: Signature find | Site
  [7:0:0.0] Great Granary, Great Bath, Pashupati seal, Dancing Girl, Priest-King | Mohenjodaro
  [7:0:0.1] Granaries in two rows of six; Cemetery R-37, H; red sandstone torso | Harappa
  [7:0:0.2] Bead factory, furnace, lipstick, ink pot; no citadel | Chanhudaro
  [7:0:0.3] Earliest ploughed field, seven fire altars, camel bones, wooden drain | Kalibangan
  [7:0:0.4] Terracotta (toy) plough, radial streets, barley | Banawali
  [7:0:0.5] Horse bones (doubtful), pot burials | Surkotada
  [7:0:0.6] Reservoirs, stadium, 10-sign signboard, three-part town, rock-cut stepwells | Dholavira
  [7:0:0.7] Bronze (copper) chariot, bronze buffalo, rhinoceros, elephant | Daimabad (CGPSC 2013)
  [7:0:0.8] Rice, bajra, sorghum; Late Harappan | Rangpur
  [7:0:0.9] Largest site in India; granary; gemstone factory | Rakhigarhi
  [7:0:0.10] Dog buried with human; oval pit burials | Ropar
  [7:0:0.11] 125 graves; human burials | Sinauli (Sanauli)
  [7:0:0.12] Earliest garlic | Balu
  [7:0:0.13] Shell objects | Nageshwar, Balakot
  [7:0:0.14] Lapis lazuli trade outpost; canals | Shortughai (Afghanistan)
  [7:0:0.15] Earliest furnace | Kot Bala
  [7:0:0.16] Salt production | Kerala-no-dhoro
  [7:0:0.17] Rhinoceros remains | Amri
  [7:0:0.18] Himalayan wood | Manda
[7:0:1] - **Mohenjodaro** = "Mound of the Dead" (Sindhi; Lothal means the same in Gujarati); **Kalibangan** = "black bangles"; Dholavira local name **Kotada**.
[7:0:2] - **Cylindrical seals:** Kalibangan and Mohenjodaro; **ship seal:** Lothal.
[7:0:3] - Surkotada horse bones dated c. 2100–1700 BCE; Banawali has pre-, mature and post-Harappan phases.
[7:0:4] > Trap: horse remains = Surkotada, not Lothal; ploughed field = Kalibangan, toy plough = Banawali.
[7:0:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.11-15; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.18-27; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.72, p.82-83; old app notes row 7
[7:0:6] - **Ropar** (Sutlej, Punjab) also gave a typical Harappan **steatite seal** with Indus script; Rakhigarhi (not Banawali) is the largest Indian site.
[7:0:7] - **Skeletons in wells and streets:** **Mohenjodaro** (late level) – Wheeler's "massacre"; now read as multiple burials.
[7:0:8] - **Earthquake:** the earliest evidence of an **earthquake** in the Harappan context is at **Kalibangan** (end of its early phase, c. 2700 BCE).
## SUB 1 | Lothal – Port Town, Dockyard and the Maritime Heritage Complex
[7:1:0] - **Lothal** (Ahmedabad, Gujarat) on the **Bhogava** (tributary of the Sabarmati), near the **Gulf of Khambhat** = **the port city** of the IVC (UPPCS 1999, BPSC 2011, SI 2019) – not Harappa, Kalibangan, Mohenjodaro or Ropar.
[7:1:1] - **Dockyard:** brick-lined basin **218 m × 37 m** with sluice gates – the world's earliest tidal dock; warehouse on a raised platform with seals and sealings.
[7:1:2] - **Discovered 1954, excavated 1955–60 by S.R. Rao** (not Wheeler, not Daya Ram Sahni).
[7:1:3] - **Other finds:** rice husk, fire altars, bead-making factory, Persian Gulf (button) seal, ship on a seal, terracotta ship, copper dog, chess-like game set, three double burials, painted jar (cunning fox story), ivory scale.
[7:1:4] - Entrance of houses on the **main street** (other cities: side lanes). Called "Mini Harappa".
[7:1:5] - **Other ports:** Rangpur, Somnath (Prabhas Patan), Balakot, Kuntasi, Khirasara, Sutkagendor.
[7:1:6] # National Maritime Heritage Complex (NMHC)
[7:1:7] - **NMHC** is being built at **Lothal**, Gujarat (not Kozhikode, Visakhapatnam, Sindhudurg, Kanyakumari, Puri or Panjim) – Ministry of Ports, Shipping and Waterways; approved by the **Union Cabinet, October 2024**.
[7:1:8] - **As of Sep 2026:** Phase 1A about **82%** complete, target **31 Oct 2026**; Phase 1B to add eight galleries and a lighthouse museum.
[7:1:9] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.12-13; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.103; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.17, p.20-21; old app notes row 7
## SUB 2 | Site → Excavator, River and State
[7:2:0] TABLE head: Site | Excavator (year) | River | Present location
  [7:2:0.0] Harappa | Daya Ram Sahni (1921) | Ravi | Punjab (Pakistan)
  [7:2:0.1] Mohenjodaro | R.D. Banerji (1922) | Indus | Larkana, Sindh (Pakistan)
  [7:2:0.2] Chanhudaro | N.G. Majumdar (1931); Mackay 1935–36 | Indus | Sindh (Pakistan)
  [7:2:0.3] Lothal | S.R. Rao (1954–57) | Bhogava | Ahmedabad, Gujarat
  [7:2:0.4] Kalibangan | A. Ghosh (1953); B.B. Lal and B.K. Thapar (1960–69) | Ghaggar | Hanumangarh, Rajasthan
  [7:2:0.5] Banawali | R.S. Bisht (1973–74) | Ghaggar (Saraswati) | Fatehabad, Haryana
  [7:2:0.6] Rakhigarhi | Amarendra Nath (1997); later Vasant Shinde | Ghaggar–Drishadvati | Hisar, Haryana
  [7:2:0.7] Dholavira | J.P. Joshi (found 1967–68); R.S. Bisht (1990) | Manhar, Mansar rivulets | Kachchh, Gujarat
  [7:2:0.8] Surkotada | J.P. Joshi (1964) | – | Kachchh, Gujarat
  [7:2:0.9] Rangpur | M.S. Vats (1931); S.R. Rao (1953–54) | Sukhbhadar | Gujarat
  [7:2:0.10] Ropar | Y.D. Sharma (1953–56) – first site dug after Independence | Sutlej | Punjab (India)
  [7:2:0.11] Daimabad | B.P. Bopardikar (1958) | Pravara | Maharashtra
  [7:2:0.12] Manda | J.P. Joshi (1982) | Chenab | Jammu
[7:2:1] - **Not in Gujarat:** **Banawali** (Haryana) – Surkotada, Lothal, Dholavira are all in Gujarat (WBCS 2018).
[7:2:2] - S.R. Rao = Lothal; R.S. Bisht = Banawali and Dholavira; J.P. Joshi = Surkotada; A. Ghosh = Kalibangan.
[7:2:3] ~~ Sources: Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.17-25; PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.82, p.111; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.4; old app notes row 7
## SUB 3 | Not a Harappan Site – Odd-one-out
[7:3:0] TABLE head: Name | Status
  [7:3:0.0] Sutkagendor (Makran, Baluchistan) | Harappan – westernmost
  [7:3:0.1] Surkotada, Desalpur (Kachchh) | Harappan
  [7:3:0.2] Kot Diji, Chanhudaro (Sindh) | Harappan (Kot Diji also pre-Harappan)
  [7:3:0.3] Rakhigarhi (Hisar) | Harappan
  [7:3:0.4] Sohgaura (Gorakhpur, UP, on the Rapti) | NOT Harappan – Mauryan-era copper plate on famine granaries (UPSC 2019, APPSC G2)
  [7:3:0.5] Pataliputra | NOT Harappan
[7:3:1] > Sohgaura sounds like Surkotada – it is a Mauryan copper-plate site.
[7:3:2] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.79-83; old app notes row 7
## SUB 4 | Largest Sites, UNESCO and Rakhigarhi
[7:4:0] - **Five largest** cities: **Mohenjodaro, Harappa, Ganweriwala, Rakhigarhi, Dholavira**.
[7:4:1] - **Rakhigarhi** (Hisar, Haryana) = **largest Harappan site** (about 350–550 ha; 11 mounds per Shinde); **Bhirrana** = oldest.
[7:4:2] TABLE head: Site | UNESCO status
  [7:4:2.0] Dholavira | WHS 2021 (India's 40th) – only inscribed Harappan site in India
  [7:4:2.1] Mohenjodaro (Pakistan) | Inscribed 1980
  [7:4:2.2] Rakhigarhi | Tentative List
[7:4:3] - **Rakhigarhi DNA:** the ~4,600-year-old female skeleton **lacked the "Steppe Pastoralist" gene** → read as **indigenous ancestry**, challenging the Aryan-invasion view.
[7:4:4] - **Mound 7** cemetery: **56 skeletons**; dig of **23 Jun 2026** found **five complete skeletons** (aged 30–40) and **three fragmentary burials**.
[7:4:5] - First site excavated: Harappa (1921); first after Independence: Ropar.
[7:4:6] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.80, p.102, p.106; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.18; old app notes row 7

=== NOTES 1:8 | Ivc Economy – Agriculture, Crops, Animals, Crafts, Trade, Seals, Weights, Meluhha
## SUB 0 | Metals and Raw Materials → Source
[8:0:0] - **Copper = the first metal used by man** (not iron, bronze, gold or silver); chalcolithic cultures began with it (APPSC G2 2011, RAS 2012).
[8:0:1] - Harappans used **copper, bronze, gold, silver, lead**; **iron NOT found** at any Harappan site (CGPSC 2011) – a Bronze Age culture.
[8:0:2] - **Bronze = copper + tin**; tools, weapons and the **Dancing Girl** (lost-wax casting). Pure copper objects far outnumber bronze ones.
[8:0:3] - Harappans **pioneered silver** use; silver imported (Afghanistan).
[8:0:4] TABLE head: Material | Source
  [8:0:4.0] Copper | Khetri mines (Rajasthan); also Oman (Magan)
  [8:0:4.1] Tin | Afghanistan, Iran
  [8:0:4.2] Gold | Karnataka (Kolar), Afghanistan
  [8:0:4.3] Lapis lazuli | Badakhshan via Shortughai (Afghanistan)
  [8:0:4.4] Carnelian | Bharuch / Saurashtra (Gujarat)
  [8:0:4.5] Steatite | South Rajasthan, north Gujarat
  [8:0:4.6] Chert, limestone (blades) | Sukkur and Rohri hills (Sindh) – mass-produced blades for Sindh (CDS 2020); not Khetri
  [8:0:4.7] Shells | Saurashtra coast, Nageshwar, Balakot
[8:0:5] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.63, p.67, p.88-90; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.5; old app notes row 8
## SUB 1 | Earliest Agriculture – Mehrgarh and the First Cereal
[8:1:0] TABLE head: First | Site / fact
  [8:1:0.0] Earliest agriculture and animal domestication in the subcontinent | Mehrgarh (Kachhi plain, Baluchistan), c. 7000 BC – not Lothal, Harappa, Mundigak, Koldihwa, Kili Gul Muhammad
  [8:1:0.1] First farmers' crops | Mainly barley, also wheat; sheep, goats, cattle
  [8:1:0.2] First cereal used by man | Barley (c. 8000 BC)
  [8:1:0.3] Early Baluchistan farming villages | Kili Gul Muhammad (c. 5000–4000 BC), Damb Sadaat, Anjira, Rana Ghundai
  [8:1:0.4] First agriculture per latest research; oldest paddy | Lahuradeva (Sant Kabir Nagar, UP)
  [8:1:0.5] Once thought oldest paddy | Koldihwa (Belan valley, UP)
  [8:1:0.6] Rice and cattle pens (not barley) | Mahagara
  [8:1:0.7] Earliest iron in India | Atranjikhera (Kali river); dug by R.C. Gaur (1962)
[8:1:1] - Mehrgarh (7th–3rd millennium BCE) is the neolithic root of the Harappan civilisation; similar villages at Mundigak (Afghanistan).
[8:1:2] - Iron spread in North India 1000–600 BC (Later Vedic).
[8:1:3] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.78; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.1, p.5; old app notes row 8
## SUB 2 | Agriculture, Crops and Cotton
[8:2:0] - **Main occupation = agriculture** (not trade or hunting); surplus stored in granaries fed the cities.
[8:2:1] TABLE head: Crop | Site of evidence
  [8:2:1.0] Wheat (largest finds) | Harappa; Mehrgarh
  [8:2:1.1] Barley | Banawali
  [8:2:1.2] Rice husk (earliest in South Asia) | Lothal, Rangpur
  [8:2:1.3] Millets and rice as staple | Rangpur, Surkotada
  [8:2:1.4] Cotton | Mohenjodaro (woven cotton piece); Mehrgarh
  [8:2:1.5] Garlic (earliest) | Balu (Haryana)
  [8:2:1.6] Indigo | Rojdi
[8:2:2] - Also: peas, lentil, chickpea, **sesame**, **mustard**, dates, melon, grapes, henna.
[8:2:3] - **Cotton:** the Harappans were the **first to grow cotton** and make cotton cloth → "India" and "cotton" are the answers (UPPSC 2006, SSC 2001); Greeks called it **Sindon** (from Sindh). Not coins – the IVC had no coins.
[8:2:4] - **Maize** came with the Portuguese. Rig Vedic economy was pastoral.
[8:2:5] # Plough and irrigation
[8:2:6] TABLE head: Evidence | Site
  [8:2:6.0] Earliest ploughed field (cross furrows, two crops together) | Kalibangan (early Harappan level)
  [8:2:6.1] Terracotta plough model | Banawali, Cholistan (Bahawalpur)
  [8:2:6.2] Clay plough | Rakhigarhi
  [8:2:6.3] Traces of canals | Shortughai (Afghanistan)
  [8:2:6.4] Well irrigation | Allahdino (Sindh)
  [8:2:6.5] Reservoirs | Dholavira
[8:2:7] - **Wooden ploughs**; flood irrigation; canal irrigation inconclusive in Punjab/Sindh; double cropping.
[8:2:8] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.86-87; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.5; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.18-26; old app notes row 8
## SUB 3 | Animals – Known and Unknown
[8:3:0] TABLE head: Status | Animals
  [8:3:0.0] Domesticated | Humped bull (zebu, favoured), oxen, buffalo, sheep, goat, pig, dog, cat, ass, camel, elephant
  [8:3:0.1] Wild but known | Elephant, tiger, rhinoceros, deer, gharial
  [8:3:0.2] Not established | Horse – no bones or images in early and mature levels; only doubtful finds (superficial level at Mohenjodaro, Lothal terracotta, Surkotada bones)
  [8:3:0.3] Never found | Lion – no remains and not on seals
  [8:3:0.4] Remains → site | Camel bones = Kalibangan; dog with human = Ropar; rhinoceros = Amri; copper rhino and elephant = Daimabad
[8:3:1] - "Animal **not known** to the IVC" = **Horse** (BPSC accepted b); "remains not discovered" with lion in the options = **Lion** (CGPSC 2011).
[8:3:2] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.87, p.96; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.5, p.14; old app notes row 8
## SUB 4 | Crafts, Industries and Standardisation
[8:4:0] TABLE head: Craft | Centre
  [8:4:0.0] Bead-making (carnelian, steatite, lapis) | Chanhudaro, Lothal
  [8:4:0.1] Shell work | Nageshwar, Balakot (specialised), Lothal, Chanhudaro
  [8:4:0.2] Black terracotta bangles | Kalibangan
  [8:4:0.3] Spinning, weaving | Mohenjodaro; Chanhudaro = "Lancashire of India"; Lothal = "Manchester of the Harappan civilisation"
  [8:4:0.4] Ivory, copper, bead workshops | Kuntasi
  [8:4:0.5] Salt | Kerala-no-dhoro
  [8:4:0.6] Earliest furnace | Kot Bala
[8:4:1] - **Chanhudaro** was almost wholly a craft town: beads, shell, metal, seals, weights.
[8:4:2] - **Spindle-whorls** common in houses; **no spinning wheel** found (UPSC 2026).
[8:4:3] - **Boat models:** Mohenjodaro (boat on seals) and Lothal (clay boats, terracotta ship).
[8:4:4] - **Kenoyer:** the amazing **standardisation** of crafts (using non-local raw materials and complex technology) was possibly due to **state control** (NDA 2015); others add hereditary craft groups and internal trade.
[8:4:5] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.88-89, p.101; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.12-13; https://byjus.com/question-answer/harappan-crafts-display-an-amazing-degree-of-standardization-according-to-kenoyer-what-was-the-possible; old app notes row 8
## SUB 5 | External Trade – Meluhha and Mesopotamia
[8:5:0] TABLE head: Mesopotamian name | Region
  [8:5:0.0] Meluhha | Indus (Harappan) region
  [8:5:0.1] Dilmun | Bahrain
  [8:5:0.2] Magan | Oman (Makran coast)
[8:5:1] - **Closest external contact = Mesopotamia** (not Egypt): Harappan seals at **Susa, Ur, Nippur**, Failaka, Bahrain; three Mesopotamian cylinder seals at Mohenjodaro; Dilmun weights followed the Harappan standard.
[8:5:2] - **Sargon of Akkad** (c. 2300 BCE) records ships from Meluhha.
[8:5:3] - **Exports:** carnelian beads, lapis lazuli, ivory, timber, cotton, copper, gold. **Imports:** silver, tin, woollens.
[8:5:4] - Contacts also with Altyn-depe (Turkmenistan), Shahr-i-Sokhta and Tepe Yahya (Iran); network across the Arabian Sea and Persian Gulf.
[8:5:5] - **Nov 2025:** a second **Dilmun temple** (c. 1900–1800 BCE) found on **Failaka**, Kuwait.
[8:5:6] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.90-92; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.3, p.6; old app notes row 8
## SUB 6 | Internal Trade and Transport
[8:6:0] - Trade by **barter**, no metal money; **bullock carts** (solid wheels) and **boats**. Reach: South India, UP, Maharashtra, Bihar, Rajasthan.
[8:6:1] - **Harappa** = inland city on the Ravi, not a port; Harappa and Mohenjodaro had warehouses.
[8:6:2] TABLE head: Port / outpost | Location
  [8:6:2.0] Lothal | Gulf of Khambhat, Gujarat – tidal dock
  [8:6:2.1] Sutkagendor | Makran coast, Baluchistan; westernmost
  [8:6:2.2] Balakot | Sindh coast; shells
  [8:6:2.3] Kuntasi, Bhagatrav, Prabhas Patan | Gujarat coast
  [8:6:2.4] Shortughai | Afghanistan – trading post for lapis
[8:6:3] - Farm surplus + trade + crafts + standard weights → **urbanisation**.
[8:6:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.89-91, p.103; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.5; old app notes row 8

[ne36c1d07099] sub0
  In which present-day country is Shortughai, a Harappan colony connected with lapis lazuli supply, located?
  Iran | Baluchistan | *Afghanistan | Oman
  x: Shortughai in Afghanistan supplied lapis lazuli from Badakhshan.
[n974eee00fd4] sub0
  From where did the Harappans obtain lapis lazuli?
  Kolar | Khetri | *Badakhshan | Oman
  x: Lapis lazuli came from Badakhshan through Shortughai; copper came from Khetri and Oman and gold from Kolar.
[n8ca449490c5] sub0
  Chert blades from which site show that stone tools continued alongside metal tools?
  Amri | *Rohri | Allahdino | Kot Bala
  x: Chert blades from Rohri show that stone tools continued alongside metal tools; iron was unknown to the Harappans.
[n9ff39b96174] sub0
  Which one of the following statements regarding silver and cotton is correct?

  x: The Harappans pioneered the use of silver and cotton. Silver is also described as an imported material in the trade evidence.
[na584cf3d969] sub0

  x: Copper came from Khetri (Rajasthan) and Oman; gold came from Kolar and lapis lazuli from Badakhshan.

  Which of the following pairs is correctly matched?
  Copper – Badakhshan | *Gold – Kolar | Lapis lazuli – Khetri | Chert – Kolar
  x: Gold came from Kolar; copper from Khetri, lapis lazuli from Badakhshan and chert from Rohri.
[g93a7da22088e] sub0
  Which metal, the first to be used by man, did the Harappans obtain from Khetri in Rajasthan?
  Tin | Gold | Silver | *Copper
  x: Copper was the first metal used by man; the Harappans got it from Khetri (Rajasthan) and Oman.
[gfc76af09197e] sub0
  Which of the following metals was NOT known to the Harappans?
  *Iron | Silver | Lead | Gold
  x: The Harappans used copper, bronze, gold, silver and lead, but iron has not been found; iron came only around 1000 BC.

  From where did the Harappans obtain tin?
  Khetri | Kolar | *Afghanistan and Iran | Saurashtra
  x: Tin came from Afghanistan and Iran; copper from Khetri and gold from Kolar.
[nc2917e83a9b] sub0
  Which of the following statements is correct regarding Harappan craft technology?
  *The Dancing Girl was made by lost-wax casting, and Lothal has yielded boat models and a terracotta ship. | Lost-wax casting was used exclusively for seals at Mohenjodaro. | The Dancing Girl was made only of terracotta, and Lothal had no evidence of boats. | Bronze objects were absent, while ships were represented only at Harappa.
  x: The Dancing Girl is a bronze object made by lost-wax casting. Lothal has yielded boat models and a terracotta ship, and ships also appear on seals.
[n15dc04d6a08] sub0

  x: Shortughai, in Afghanistan, was a trading colony for lapis lazuli. Sutkagendor was on the Makran coast, Balakot on the Sindh coast and Lothal in Gujarat.
[nindian-history-34-5] sub1
  Which pair of food grains is linked with the earliest farming evidence at Mehrgarh and at Lahuradeva respectively?
  Paddy and Barley | Millet and Wheat | Maize and Paddy | *Barley and Paddy
  x: Mehrgarh's earliest farmers grew mainly barley, while Lahuradeva in the Ganga plain gives the oldest evidence of paddy (rice). Reversing the pair or naming millet or maize is wrong.
[nindian-history-38-4] sub1
  Which of the following statements is incorrect?
  Lahuradeva has the oldest evidence of paddy cultivation. | *Mahagara provides early evidence of barley cultivation. | Uraiyur is associated with cotton trade. | Koldihwa was earlier considered to have the oldest evidence of paddy cultivation.
  x: Option B is incorrect: the earliest barley evidence comes from Mehrgarh, while Mahagara is known for rice and cattle pens. Lahuradeva has the oldest paddy evidence, Koldihwa was once thought oldest, and Uraiyur traded cotton.
[nindian-history-35-6] sub1
  Assertion (A): Iron spread in North India between 1000 and 600 BC.
Reason (R): The Iron Age in India is associated with the Later Vedic period, around 1000 BC.
  Both A and R are true, and R is the correct explanation of A | *Both A and R are true, but R is NOT the correct explanation of A | A is true, but R is false | A is false, but R is true
  x: Both are true but R does not explain A: iron spread in North India during 1000–600 BC, and the Iron Age began in the Later Vedic period. R gives the start date, not the reason for the spread.

  x: The earliest iron in India is from Atranjikhera on the Kali river, dug by R.C. Gaur in 1962.

  Banawali | *Kalibangan | Lothal | Surkotada

[na6c898d6c68] sub2

  x: Garlic is evidenced at Balu; Lothal and Rangpur are the sites for rice.
[n18092cc42d7] sub2
  Which of the following pairs is NOT correctly matched?
  *Chanhudaro — earliest evidence of rice cultivation in Gujarat | Balu — earliest evidence of garlic | Rojdi — indigo | Rangpur and Surkotada — millets and rice as staple crops
  x: Chanhudaro is associated with cotton textiles and craft production. The earliest evidence of rice cultivation in Gujarat is at Lothal, dated to approximately 1800 BCE.
[nfe1fba7de0d] sub2
  Which of the following groups consists only of crops cultivated by the Harappans?
  Tea, coffee, rubber, wheat and barley | Maize, potato, tobacco, cotton and indigo | Jute, sugarcane, tea, rice and coffee | *Wheat, barley, peas, sesame and mustard
  x: Wheat, barley, peas, sesame and mustard are among the crops cultivated by the Harappans. The other groups contain crops not included in the given evidence.
[n8d027806cfc] sub2
  Which one of the following statements about the Rig Vedic economy is correct?

  x: The Rig Vedic economy was pastoral, and cotton, rice and wheat are not mentioned in the Rig Veda.
[nindian-history-38-10] sub2
  Which Harappan crop was connected with textiles and trade?
  Maize | Jute | *Cotton | Indigo
  x: Cotton was the Harappan textile crop; maize was unknown in India until the Portuguese brought it.
[ndae12627ff6] sub2
  What did the Greeks call cotton?
  *Sindon | Karpasa | Kapas | Muslin
  x: The Greeks called cotton Sindon; the Harappans pioneered cotton textiles.

  x: Kalibangan has yielded the earliest ploughed field with furrows indicating mixed cropping. Banawali gave a terracotta plough model, Lothal is known for its dockyard and rice husks, and Allahdino for well irrigation.

=== NOTES 1:9 | Ivc Seals, Weights and Measures, Harappan Script
## SUB 0 | Seals and Sealings – Uses and Value As A Source
[9:0:0] - The seal is the **most distinctive artefact** of the IVC (APPSC FRO; APPSC GS 2024) – not megaliths, DNA or royal palaces.
[9:0:1] - With the script unread, **seals** are the **main source** of knowledge about the Harappan people (RAS 1996); broadly, the culture is known only from **archaeological excavations** (UPPSC 1993) – no inscriptions read, no religious books.
[9:0:2] - A **sealing** is the clay impression of a seal pressed on the knot of a package; an intact sealing proved the goods were untouched and named the sender.
[9:0:3] - **Uses:** facilitating **long-distance communication** and trade (APPSC G1 → **seals and sealings**), authentication, **ownership**, amulets – **not coins**.
[9:0:4] - **Uniform seals and weights** = main proof of **commercial and economic development** and regulated trade (SSC CPO 2002).
[9:0:5] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.96; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.6; old app notes row 9
## SUB 1 | Seal Motifs and Famous Seals
[9:1:0] TABLE head: Shown on seals / terracotta | Not shown
  [9:1:0.0] Unicorn (most common), humped bull (best artwork), bull, buffalo, elephant, tiger, rhinoceros, bison, goat, antelope, gharial | Cow, horse, lion, camel (PMF memory aid "HoLiCa")
[9:1:1] - "Animal **not** on seals and terracotta" with cow in the options = **Cow** (UPSC 2001); with horse = **Horse** (UPPCS 2009); lion is never shown either.
[9:1:2] - **Pipal** = the tree most commonly depicted on seals.
[9:1:3] - **Pashupati seal** (Mohenjodaro, steatite): three-faced horned figure in yogic posture ringed by **elephant, tiger, rhinoceros and buffalo**, two deer below.
[9:1:4] TABLE head: Seal | Site
  [9:1:4.0] Pashupati seal | Mohenjodaro
  [9:1:4.1] Famous (humped) bull seal | Mohenjodaro (Rocket; old notes) – printed keys often say Harappa
  [9:1:4.2] Unicorn seal, first seal found (Cunningham, 1872–75) | Harappa
  [9:1:4.3] Seal with unicorn + script | Nippur (Mesopotamia)
  [9:1:4.4] Mesopotamian-type cylinder seals | Mohenjodaro, Kalibangan
  [9:1:4.5] Button / Persian Gulf-type seal | Lothal
[9:1:5] - **Jun 2026:** cylindrical **steatite** seal at **Rakhigarhi** – **alligator motif** and **five** Harappan characters; chert weights also found.
[9:1:6] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.96, p.108; Rocket Indian History Part 1 p.19; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.6-7; old app notes row 9
## SUB 2 | Weights and Measures
[9:2:0] - Weights mostly **chert** (also limestone, steatite), **cubical** (not spherical), no markings.
[9:2:1] TABLE head: Denomination | System
  [9:2:1.0] Lower | Binary: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64
  [9:2:1.1] Higher | Decimal multiples: 160, 320, 640, 1600, 3200
[9:2:2] - Unit weight about **13.63 g**; "16" ratio (16 annas = 1 rupee tradition).
[9:2:3] - Same weights over about **8,00,000 sq km** → **central regulation** of trade; Dilmun weights followed this standard.
[9:2:4] TABLE head: Scale | Site | Unit
  [9:2:4.0] Ivory scale | Lothal (APPSC G2 2011) | Smallest division ~1.70 mm
  [9:2:4.1] Shell scale | Mohenjodaro | ~6.7 mm
  [9:2:4.2] Copper scale | Harappa | –
[9:2:5] - Foot ≈ 37.6 cm; cubit 51.8–53.6 cm. Ivory weight balance at Mohenjodaro and Lothal.
[9:2:6] > Ivory = Lothal; shell = Mohenjodaro; copper = Harappa.
[9:2:7] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.95; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.9; old app notes row 9
## SUB 3 | Seals – Material and Shape
[9:3:0] - Mostly **steatite (soapstone)** – easy to carve; also agate, chert, copper, faience, terracotta, ivory.
[9:3:1] - Standard seal: **square** plaque about 2 × 2 inches of steatite.
[9:3:2] TABLE head: Shape | Feature / site
  [9:3:2.0] Square | Animal motif + inscription
  [9:3:2.1] Rectangular | Inscription only
  [9:3:2.2] Mesopotamian-type cylinder | Rare; Mohenjodaro, Kalibangan
  [9:3:2.3] Button seal, Persian Gulf-type | Lothal
[9:3:3] > Cylinder seals are Mesopotamian-type and rare; the normal Harappan seal is square steatite.
[9:3:4] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.96; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.6; old app notes row 9
## SUB 4 | Harappan Script – Features
[9:4:0] TABLE head: Feature | Fact
  [9:4:0.0] Nature | Mostly pictographic (not alphabetic); fish = commonest sign
  [9:4:0.1] Direction | Right to left; some boustrophedon (Kalibangan)
  [9:4:0.2] Number of signs | About 375–400 (estimates 62 to 676)
  [9:4:0.3] Average / longest inscription | About 5 / 26 signs
  [9:4:0.4] Corpus | About 5,000 inscribed objects (≈3,500 seals)
  [9:4:0.5] Found on | Seals, tablets, pottery, copper tools, bone rods, jewellery, a signboard
  [9:4:0.6] Status | Undeciphered
[9:4:1] - First noticed 1853; seals with unknown symbols reported by **J. Fleet, 1912**.
[9:4:2] - Unread because **no bilingual text** (like the **Rosetta Stone**) has been found and the language is unknown.
[9:4:3] - **Dholavira signboard:** **ten large signs** near the **North Gate** – largest Harappan letters; Dholavira dug by **R.S. Bisht, 1990**.
[9:4:4] # Decipherment views
[9:4:5] TABLE head: Hypothesis | Scholar
  [9:4:5.0] Proto-Dravidian (most supported) | Iravatham Mahadevan, Asko Parpola, Father Heras
  [9:4:5.1] Indo-Aryan (ancestor of Sanskrit) | S.R. Rao (minority view)
  [9:4:5.2] Not a language script (tax / trade marks) | Bahata Ansumali Mukhopadhyay
[9:4:6] ~~ Sources: PMF Ancient & Medieval Part 1 p.92-95; NextGen Indian History - APPSC Exams Part 1 p.1, p.7; old app notes row 9

  What was the usual shape of Harappan weights, which were mostly made of chert?
  cylindrical | spherical | conical | *cubical
  x: Harappan weights were generally cubical, not spherical.
[n2c349bd0de9] sub2
  Which of the following represents the numerical system of Harappan weights?
  A sexagesimal system without binary subdivisions | Only a decimal system from the smallest denomination onward | A random system varying from site to site | *Binary values in lower denominations followed by decimal multiples
  x: Lower denominations followed the binary sequence 1:2:4:8:16:32:64, after which decimal multiples such as 160, 320 and 640 were used.
[f7aeb0227b900] sub2
  What was the approximate Harappan unit weight (the '16' ratio)?
  6.7 g | 1.70 g | *13.63 g | 27.5 g
  x: The Harappan unit weight was about 13.63 g.
[fd4570506daf3] sub2
  Identical Harappan weights found over about how large an area point to central regulation of trade?
  1,00,000 sq km | 2,50,000 sq km | *8,00,000 sq km | 15,00,000 sq km
  x: The same weights are found over about 8,00,000 sq km, pointing to central regulation of trade.
[fbd69590d6c7d] sub2
  The higher denominations of Harappan weights followed which system?
  Binary | *Decimal | Sexagesimal | Duodecimal
  x: Lower weights were binary (1 to 64); higher weights were decimal multiples such as 160, 320 and 640.
[f7b2a312e0b40] sub2
  What is the approximate unit of the shell scale of Mohenjodaro?
  1.70 mm | *6.7 mm | 13.63 mm | 46 mm
  x: The shell scale from Mohenjodaro has a unit of about 6.7 mm.
[fe8827eb9e81a] sub2
  Which pair of Harappan scale and site is correctly matched?
  *Copper scale – Harappa | Ivory scale – Mohenjodaro | Shell scale – Lothal | Ivory scale – Harappa
  x: Ivory scale = Lothal, shell scale = Mohenjodaro, copper scale = Harappa.
[g8073d9b973d1] sub2
  Of which material was the measuring scale found at Lothal made?
  Shell | Copper | *Ivory | Bone
  x: Lothal yielded an ivory scale; the shell scale is from Mohenjodaro and the copper one from Harappa.
[g98d3ed877a82] sub2
  What was the smallest division on the ivory scale of Lothal?
  *About 1.70 mm | About 6.7 mm | About 13.63 mm | About 46 mm
  x: The Lothal ivory scale has 27 divisions in about 46 mm, each about 1.70 mm.
[gb425864a94ee] sub2
  At which Harappan site was an ivory scale found?
  Kalibangan | Banawali | *Lothal | Ropar
  x: Lothal yielded an ivory scale besides its dockyard and bead factory.
[nc103fdb8efe] sub3
  What do rectangular Harappan seals generally carry?
  *Only inscriptions | An animal motif with an inscription | Only animal motifs | Human figures only
  x: Rectangular seals carry only inscriptions, while square seals combine an animal motif with an inscription.
[f0b7289438575] sub3
  Most Harappan seals were of which shape?
  Rectangular | *Square | Cylindrical | Button
  x: Most seals were square, carrying an animal and script; rectangular seals carry script only.
[nc38866c0efb] sub3
  Which of the following is NOT correct about Harappan seals?
  *Mesopotamian-type cylinder seals were common at all sites. | Rectangular seals generally carry only an inscription. | Square seals generally carry an animal figure with an inscription. | Most seals were made of steatite.
  x: Mesopotamian-type cylinder seals were rare, not common, at Harappan sites.
[n3500296503a] sub3
  Of which material were Harappan seals mostly made?
  Ivory | *Steatite | Copper | Gold
  x: Harappan seals were mostly of steatite and generally square.
[g61db1fbbef04] sub3
  Besides steatite, which material was also used for Harappan seals?
  Iron | Glass | *Terracotta | Marble
  x: Seals were mostly of steatite, but also of terracotta, copper and ivory.
[fa503c1a5cada] sub3
  Of which material were most Harappan seals made?
  *Steatite | Terracotta | Agate | Chert
  x: Seals were mostly of steatite; a few were of terracotta, agate and chert.
[f19ccd3abfe91] sub3
  What was the commonest shape of Harappan seals?
  Cylindrical | *Square | Circular | Cube
  x: Most seals were square; a few were cube, circular or cylindrical.
[nd9b23a69602] sub4
  Where has the largest Indus Valley inscription, a ten-sign signboard, been reported from?
  Citadel of Mohenjodaro | Dockyard of Lothal | *North Gate of Dholavira | South Gate of Harappa
  x: The largest inscription is the ten-sign signboard found at the North Gate of Dholavira.
[n14456ec5b08] sub4
  What is the usual direction of writing in the Harappan script?
  Top to bottom only | Left to right only | Bottom to top | *Right to left
  x: Harappan inscriptions were generally written from right to left, as indicated partly by cramped spacing at the left end.
[n96287c57cae] sub4
  The average Harappan inscription contains about how many signs?
  About 10 | About 26 | *About 5 | About 50
  x: The average Harappan inscription has only about five signs; the script remains undeciphered.
[n6601ab64da1] sub4
  Which of the following is NOT correct about the Harappan script?
  It is mainly pictographic. | It has approximately 400 signs. | It remains undeciphered. | *It was deciphered through bilingual inscriptions.
  x: The script is pictographic with about 400 signs and is still undeciphered, so it was never read through bilingual inscriptions.
[n6c361d80641] sub4
  Why does the Harappan script remain undeciphered?
  No Harappan inscriptions have survived | *No bilingual text has been found | The script was written only on perishable materials | All inscriptions are in a single long text
  x: The Harappan script is still undeciphered because no bilingual text (like the Rosetta Stone) has been discovered.
[f4ccb0d40d317] sub4
  Who first noticed the Harappan script in 1912?
  S.R. Rao | *J. Fleet | R.S. Bisht | Iravatham Mahadevan
  x: J. Fleet first noticed the Indus script in 1912.
[fd5b44d277c8e] sub4
  From which site is the boustrophedon style of Harappan writing reported?
  *Kalibangan | Lothal | Dholavira | Rakhigarhi
  x: The script is usually written right to left, with some boustrophedon writing at Kalibangan.
[g14f3202ea2dc] sub4
  About how many inscribed Harappan objects are known?
  500 | 1,000 | *5,000 | 50,000
  x: The Indus script corpus has about 5,000 inscribed objects.
[n67f1a4d0e0d] sub4
  Which hypothesis on the Harappan script is correctly matched with its scholar(s)?
  *Proto-Dravidian – Iravatham Mahadevan and Asko Parpola | Proto-Dravidian – S. R. Rao | Indo-Aryan (early Sanskrit) – Asko Parpola | Indo-Aryan (early Sanskrit) – Iravatham Mahadevan
  x: The Proto-Dravidian hypothesis is associated with Iravatham Mahadevan and Asko Parpola; the Indo-Aryan view with S. R. Rao.
[fd86b1e6b02b8] sub4
  How did S.R. Rao read the Harappan script?
  Proto-Dravidian | *Early Indo-Aryan (Sanskrit) | Pictographic Sumerian | Undeciphered and unreadable
  x: S.R. Rao held the Indo-Aryan view, a minority view; Mahadevan and Parpola favour Proto-Dravidian.
[f0b90376d3df3] sub4
  Along with Iravatham Mahadevan, which scholar supports the Proto-Dravidian reading of the Harappan script?
  S.R. Rao | J. Fleet | *Asko Parpola | R.S. Bisht
  x: Iravatham Mahadevan and Asko Parpola support the Proto-Dravidian hypothesis, the most widely supported view.

  10 signs | 5 signs | 40 signs | *26 signs
  x: The longest Harappan text has 26 signs; the Dholavira signboard (10) is the largest in sign size.

  1 and 2 only | *2 and 3 only | 1 and 3 only | 1, 2 and 3
  x: The script is pictographic, written right to left with about 400+ signs, and remains undeciphered.

  Alphabetic | *Pictographic | Syllabic, like Brahmi | Derived from Kharosthi
